Every used arrow was restriction, duality, or a localization boundary, so the result concerns the actual restriction map. ∎

C.10. A cell criterion for the actual Ext-to-Hom isomorphism

Let \(X\) have the proper finite linear \(T\)-cell filtration of §5. Let \(A=H_T^*(X,E)\). For actual equivariant bounded constructible objects \(P,Q\), assume:

  1. On every cell \(C\), the cohomology sheaves of \(i_C^*P\) and \(i_C^!Q\) are geometrically constant and are concentrated, respectively, in two fixed parities \(\eta_P,\eta_Q\).
  2. For every cell origin \(x\), the actual map \(H_T^*(X,P)\to H_T^*(x,P_x)\) is surjective.
  3. Restriction from each cell to its origin identifies \(H_T^*(C,i_C^*P)\) with \(H_T^*(x,P_x)\).

The third condition also follows from the linear cell contraction whenever that contraction has the indicated actual equivariance. The theorem does not assert that stalk parity supplies the second condition.

Theorem C.10.1.1. Under these hypotheses, equivariant cohomology induces an isomorphism \[ h:\operatorname{Ext}_{D_T(X)}^*(P,Q) \xrightarrow{\sim} \operatorname{Hom}_A^*(H_T^*(X,P),H_T^*(X,Q)). \tag{C.10.1} \] For a family satisfying these conditions for its pairs, the isomorphisms preserve identities and actual Yoneda composition.

Proof. We prove a strengthened statement simultaneously for every term \(X'\) of the closed filtration: the comparison for \((i_{X'}^*P,i_{X'}^!Q)\) is an isomorphism with the original \(A\)-linear Hom, and its Ext module is finite free over \(S\), concentrated in parity \(\eta_Q-\eta_P\). The empty term starts the induction. For a last open cell \(u:C\to X'\) and closed complement \(v:Z\to X'\), set \(E'=i_{X'}^*P\) and \(F'=i_{X'}^!Q\).

The smaller pair is exactly \((i_Z^*P,i_Z^!Q)\), by composition of the respective restrictions. Its cell hypotheses are inherited. The original surjection to any origin in \(Z\) factors through \(H_T^*(Z,i_Z^*P)\), so the recursive source has the required origin surjectivity. Every module action throughout this strengthened induction is through the original \(A\).

First record the cell calculation. A geometrically constant equivariant cohomology system on \(C\) is the constant coefficient system with its vector space: connected \(T\) gives identity on its cohomology, as in C.9. Equivariant cohomology of the constant object on the representation \(C\) is \(S\), and its compact cohomology is \(S(-d)[-2d]\), by the vector-bundle contraction and smooth trace. A complex whose cohomology lies in one parity splits in the geometric equivariant derived category into these shifted constant systems. Indeed each Postnikov attaching obstruction is an odd Ext between constant systems, whereas \(H_T^*(C,E)=S\) has no odd degrees. This proves the assertion inductively through its finite cohomology range. No splitting of Frobenius representations is needed.

Thus ordinary and compact cell cohomology are finite free \(S\)-modules of their specified parity. Between the two cell complexes, the actual Ext-to-cohomology map is the usual matrix map with entries in \(S\); it is an isomorphism. To check this without depending on a splitting choice, check constant shifted summands: cup multiplication sends an Ext entry \(a\in S\) to multiplication by \(a\). Finite direct sums give the matrix assertion, and naturality makes the conclusion independent of the chosen summand presentations. The action of \(A\) on these modules factors through \(H_T^*(C,E)=S\). That map is onto, since the base inclusion \(S\to A\) restricts to the identity. Hence their \(A\)-linear Hom equals their \(S\)-linear Hom.

Next use compact open–closed induction for the *restrictions of \(P\). All terms have parity \(\eta_P\), so the connecting maps of degree one are zero. This proves finite freeness and the exact first filtration of C.2, \[ 0\to H_{T,c}^*(C,u^*E')\to H_T^*(X',E') \to H_T^*(Z,v^*E')\to0. \tag{C.10.2} \] The properness of \(X'\) identifies ordinary and compact images here. Freeness follows by lifting a basis of the free cell or quotient module to split each \(S\)-module extension.

Similarly, closed-costalk induction for \(F'\) uses \(v^!F'\), whose costalk parity is inherited by composition of exceptional restrictions. The open cell terms have parity \(\eta_Q\). It proves freeness and \[ 0\to H_T^*(Z,v^!F')\to H_T^*(X',F') \to H_T^*(C,u^*F')\to0. \tag{C.10.3} \]

Hypotheses 2–3 supply surjectivity \(H_T^*(X',E')\to H_T^*(C,u^*E')\). In fact the surjection from \(X\) to the cell origin factors through this map and then through the isomorphism in hypothesis 3. The supported triangle and surjectivity in every degree consequently give \[ 0\to H_T^*(Z,v^!E')\to H_T^*(X',E') \to H_T^*(C,u^*E')\to0. \tag{C.10.4} \] This step uses no unproved exceptional-parity assertion for \(i_{X'}^*P\).

The three sequences (C.10.2)–(C.10.4) are exactly those of C.2. Identify their localized supports separately. For the compact open and ordinary open modules \(P_c,P_U,F_U\), the cup action factors through \[ A\longrightarrow H_T^*(C,E)=S \longrightarrow H_T^*(x,E)=S, \tag{C.10.6} \] where \(x\) is the last cell origin. After localization this is the character of that origin in \(\prod_{y\in P}Q\), so these modules are supported there, or are zero. For \(P_!,P_Z,F_Z\), apply Theorem C.5.2.1 to their actual closed extensions from \(Z\) into the proper original \(X\); their localized supports lie among the complementary origins. This does not assert vanishing boundary stalks for an ordinary open direct image. The target modules in C.2 are free by (C.10.3) and the cell calculation. Lemmas C.1–C.2 therefore give the actual left-exact Hom row.

Apply \(\operatorname{RHom}(-,F')\) to \(u_!u^*E'\to E'\to v_*v^*E'\to\). Closed and open adjunction identify its two outer terms with \[ \operatorname{Ext}_{D_T(Z)}^*(v^*E',v^!F'), \qquad \operatorname{Ext}_{D_T(C)}^*(u^*E',u^*F'). \tag{C.10.5} \] By the strengthened induction and the cell calculation they are finite free \(S\)-modules, concentrated in parity \(\eta_Q-\eta_P\). The degree-one connecting maps are zero, giving the genuine short exact upper Ext row. Its free cell quotient has a homogeneous \(S\)-basis; lifting that basis splits the sequence as \(S\)-modules. The middle Ext module is therefore finite free with the same parity, proving this part of the strengthened assertion. Freeness has been proved from the Ext sequence, rather than inferred from a Hom submodule of free modules.

Its maps and the lower Hom maps form the commutative diagram of C.3: the closed arrow is the counit on the target and unit on the source, and the open arrow is actual restriction. Applying cohomology gives precisely the factorization defining \(\alpha,\beta\) in C.2.

The outer comparison maps are isomorphisms by the induction and cell calculation. Lemma C.3.1.1 makes the middle comparison an isomorphism, and also proves the lower row surjective. The empty union starts the induction. At \(X'=X\) this is (C.10.1).

Finally \(h\) is induced by the actual cohomology functor. Its map on a composite of derived morphisms is the composite of its maps, with the ordinary grading and shift convention; it sends an identity to an identity. Thus the theorem preserves Yoneda products, rather than only computing their dimensions. ∎

Lemma C.5.1.1 and Theorem C.5.2.1 prove the displayed Euler annihilation and localized algebra. Lemmas C.1–C.3 give the exact Hom row and integral lifting. Theorem C.10.1.1 verifies those rows and composition under its stated fixed-point restriction hypotheses. Localization is used to prove vanishing; the upper Ext surjection supplies the lifts. Editable SVG source.

C.11. Three solved exercises

Exercise C.11.1 (basic). Describe \(\Omega_1\) for \(GL_1\), and compute its contraction over \(R=k[\epsilon]/\epsilon^2\).

Solution. The lattice quotient is the line generated by \(1+at^{-1}\) in \(t^{-1}O/tO\). Its positive projection is the isomorphism sending that vector to \(1\). Multiplication by \(t\) preserves the line precisely when \[ a=a(1+at^{-1}),\qquad a^2=0. \] Thus the chart is \(\operatorname{Spec}k[a]/a^2\), with normalized negative loop \(1+at^{-1}\) and inverse \(1-at^{-1}\). Over the stated dual numbers, \(a=b\epsilon\) with arbitrary \(b\in k\). The monoid sends \(a\) to \(sa\). Its zero map is the identity lattice, but the chart itself has its nonzero tangent direction. This verifies both the sign and retention of the nilpotent parameter in C.6–C.7.

Exercise C.11.2 (intermediate). For \(T=\mathbb G_m\), compute the annihilating Euler polynomial for \(V=k_\chi\oplus k_{2\chi}\). Compute the localized support of a closed-extension point module inside a two-fixed-point proper cell scheme.

Solution. With \(u=c_1(\chi)\), the character-line calculation gives \(e_T(V)=u(2u)=2u^2\), of degree \(4\), with coefficient twist \(E(2)\). It is nonzero over the characteristic-zero field \(E\), also when the geometric characteristic is two; the integral character \(2\chi\) has not become a zero coefficient in \(E\). Lemma C.5.1.1 kills both images from \(V\setminus0\) after inverting \(u\).

In the ring \(A=S[h]/h(h-u)\) of §4, the point modules have localized supports \(e_0=1-h/u\) and \(e_\infty=h/u\), respectively. Each idempotent acts as identity on its own point module and zero on the other. Thus C.1 gives their integral cross-Hom vanishing. At \(u=0\) their specialized-module Hom is instead \(E\), exactly as computed in §4; this does not contradict localization or prove that Hom commutes with specialization.

Exercise C.11.3 (advanced). Let \(X=\mathbf P^1\), with affine open \(j:C=\mathbf A^1_\alpha\to X\) and its nonzero \(T\)-weight \(\alpha\). Show that \(F=j_!E_C\) has even ordinary cell restrictions, while \(H_T^*(X,F)\to H_T^*(C,E)\) is not onto. Explain the relevance to recursive closed restriction of an IC.

Solution. Its open restriction is \(E_C\) in degree zero and its closed ordinary restriction is zero. Both satisfy even ordinary parity. Properness gives \[ H_T^*(X,F)=H_{T,c}^*(C,E)=S(-1)[-2], \qquad H_T^*(C,E)=S. \] The restriction map is multiplication by the character Euler class \[ S(-1)[-2]\xrightarrow{\alpha}S. \tag{C.11.1} \] To determine this actual arrow, the zero-section orientation maps \(S(-1)[-2]\) to the compact cohomology of the line. Its composite with the line trace is the identity by Lesson 6 A.1.1.1, hence is the compact orientation isomorphism. Forgetting compact support and restricting to the zero section sends that class to \(c_1(k_\alpha)=\alpha\), by A.1.2.1. This proves (C.11.1) with its trace normalization.

The map is injective and its cokernel is \(S/(\alpha)\), so it is not onto. The localization boundary places this cokernel in odd supported cohomology at infinity. Thus ordinary parity does not imply exceptional parity or open-restriction surjectivity.

For a recursively restricted source \(i_{X'}^*P\), its ordinary cell restrictions are inherited from \(P\), but its exceptional restriction to a smaller closed union is \(v^!i_{X'}^*P\); it need not be the exceptional restriction of \(P\) to that union in the original space. The common-parity theorem for the original IC therefore does not prove the missing condition 2 of C.10. The weight lemma C.9 supplies a distinct sufficient implication only under its explicitly stated hypothesis \((W_c)\).

C.12. Restriction from the whole Grassmannian

The exact earlier proof inputs are Lesson 1, Theorem 2.C, Lesson 2, Theorem 7.1 and Lemmas 7.10-7.12, Lesson 4, Theorem 3.5 and section 5, and Lesson 5, Lemma Q.1.2.1 and the closed-open triangles of Appendices O-P. They supply the root-coordinate recursion, full bounded stages, closure and component classification, and normalized affine-line trace. The restriction and inverse-limit argument are proved below.

Continue over an algebraically closed field \(k\), with the original connected reductive group \(G\), maximal torus \(T\), and Borel \(B\). The coefficient field \(E\) has characteristic zero and residue characteristic different from that of \(k\). Put \[ \Lambda=X_*(T),\qquad Q^\vee=\mathbb Z\Phi^\vee,\qquad \Pi=\Lambda/Q^\vee,\qquad S=H_T^*(\mathrm{pt},E). \] The quotient \(\Pi\) includes the free directions contributed by the centre. We use the actual full bounded stages \(Y_N\) of Lesson 2, Theorem 7.1 and §7.6, with their original scheme structures. Ordinary cohomology on this countable ind-presentation means derived compatible sections on those stages. We will compute that complex explicitly below. In particular, we will prove that its restriction to a finite support is onto; this assertion is not built into the definition.

C.12.1. Cells of bounded dimension in one component

Let \(I\subset G(k[[t]])\) be the inverse image of \(B\) under evaluation at zero. For a root \(\alpha\), set \(r(\alpha)=0\) for \(\alpha>0\), and \(r(\alpha)=1\) for \(\alpha<0\).

Lemma C.12.1.1. The \(I\)-orbit \(C_\lambda=It^\lambda K/K\), for \(\lambda\in\Lambda\), is a \(T\)-linear affine cell of dimension \[ d_I(\lambda)= \sum_{\alpha\in\Phi} \max\bigl(0,\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle-r(\alpha)\bigr). \tag{C.12.1} \] It is contained in the actual negative-loop open \(U_\lambda\) of C.7. Its contraction there acts on the coordinate indexed by \((\alpha,n)\) as \[ c_{\alpha,n}\longmapsto s^{\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle-n}c_{\alpha,n}, \qquad r(\alpha)\le n<\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle. \tag{C.12.2} \] In each fixed class \(\kappa\in\Pi\), only finitely many such cells have \(d_I(\lambda)\le D\), for every integer \(D\).

Proof. The ordered root-coordinate and height argument of Lesson 1, Theorem 2.C, applies to \(I/(I\cap t^\lambda Kt^{-\lambda})\) as follows. In the big-cell coordinates of \(I\), conjugation by \(t^{-\lambda}\) changes the \(\alpha\)-coordinate exponent \(n\) to \(n-\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\). The integral big-cell test in that theorem shows that the conjugated element is integral exactly when each root coordinate has valuation at least \(\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\). The torus arc is unchanged. Thus the stabilizer has root thresholds \[ \max\bigl(r(\alpha),\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\bigr), \] and contains every torus arc.

Here is the coefficient split needed for this quotient. Place first the finitely many coefficients with \(r(\alpha)\le n<\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\), and then the common coefficients at or above both thresholds. Each set is closed under addition whenever the sum is a real affine root. Two of the first set cannot sum to a positive imaginary degree: for opposite roots their exponents have sum at least one, while the two strict upper inequalities would make that sum negative. Common coefficients and their opposite-root torus corrections stay in the common set.

Use the positive integral affine height from the proof of Theorem 2.C to reorder these products. A commutator changes only coefficients of larger height. Opposite-root interchanges have denominator \(1+uv\) with \(uv\in tR[[t]]\); its inverse is the convergent geometric series, and its torus correction is common. At each height recover the next coefficient uniquely by subtracting the already known corrections. At any bounded height these operations and their inverses are polynomial in finitely many coefficients. This proves, over every test algebra \(R\), the unique factorization into the displayed first coefficients and a stabilizer element. The quotient is their affine space, giving (C.12.1). The refined Cartan decomposition in the same theorem identifies these cells, without repetitions, as the cells of the Grassmannian.

A representative of this affine space is an ordered product of \(u_\alpha(c_{\alpha,n}t^n)t^\lambda\). Move \(t^\lambda\) to the left. All remaining factors have exponent \(n-\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle<0\), are polynomial in \(t^{-1}\), and have value one at infinity. Their product is an actual element of \(L^{--}G(R)\). This proves the claimed inclusion in \(U_\lambda\), including its scheme structure. Substitution \(t^{-1}\mapsto st^{-1}\) gives (C.12.2); every displayed exponent of \(s\) is positive. Constant \(T\) conjugation multiplies this coordinate by the character \(\alpha\). Therefore these are linear \(T\)-coordinates, and the two actions commute.

For a positive root put \(m=\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\). Its pair with \(-\alpha\) contributes \[ \max(0,m)+\max(0,-m-1) = \begin{cases}m,&m\ge0,\\-m-1,&m<0.\end{cases} \] Consequently \[ |\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle|\le d_I(\lambda)+1. \tag{C.12.3} \] Fix a representative \(\lambda_0\) of \(\kappa\). Then \(\lambda-\lambda_0\in Q^\vee\). The simple-root pairings give an injective real linear map on the real span of the coroots: its matrix in the simple-coroot basis is the invertible finite Cartan matrix, as proved by the positive definite root-space form in Lesson 1, Theorem 2.C. Inequality (C.12.3) bounds all these pairings, so it bounds \(\lambda-\lambda_0\) in that real vector space. A bounded set meets the free lattice \(Q^\vee\) in finitely many elements, by expressing it in an integral basis. This proves finiteness. If \(G\) is a torus, \(Q^\vee=0\) and each class has exactly one cell; the same argument includes this case. ∎

C.12.2. The full stages and reduced Schubert exhaustion

Write \(\operatorname{Gr}_\kappa\) for the component with class \(\kappa\). The classification and local constancy of this class were proved in Lesson 4, §5, for the original group and in every geometric characteristic.

Lemma C.12.2.1. Each component has a nested sequence of projective reduced Schubert varieties \(Z_j\) such that every reduced bounded stage \(Y_{N,\kappa,\mathrm{red}}\) is contained in some \(Z_j\), and every \(Z_j\) is contained in some full bounded stage. The actual restrictions \[ R\Gamma_T(Y_{N,\kappa},E)\longrightarrow R\Gamma_T(Y_{N,\kappa,\mathrm{red}},E) \tag{C.12.4} \] are multiplicative quasi-isomorphisms, compatible with all transitions.

Proof. Choose a dominant \(\lambda_0\) in the class \(\kappa\); reflecting any representative to a dominant one preserves its class, since \(s_\alpha\lambda-\lambda=-\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\alpha^\vee\). Put \[ \eta=\sum_{\alpha>0}\alpha^\vee,\qquad \lambda_j=\lambda_0+j\eta,\qquad Z_j=\overline{\operatorname{Gr}^{\lambda_j}}_{\mathrm{red}}. \] Each simple-coroot coefficient of \(\eta\) is positive: the positive coroots have nonnegative coefficients, and the corresponding simple coroot occurs among them. A simple reflection permutes all positive coroots except its own simple coroot, which it makes negative. Hence \(s_i\eta=\eta-2\alpha_i^\vee\), so \(\langle\alpha_i,\eta\rangle=2\). Thus every \(\lambda_j\) is dominant and \(\lambda_j\le\lambda_{j+1}\). Lesson 4, Theorem 3.5 proves that the \(Z_j\) are nested and projective.

If \(\mu\) is any dominant coweight of class \(\kappa\), then \(\lambda_0-\mu\) has integral simple-coroot coefficients. Adding \(j\eta\), whose coefficients are all positive, makes them all nonnegative for sufficiently large \(j\). Thus \(\mu\le\lambda_j\). For a torus the class has only \(\mu=\lambda_0\), and the sequence is constant.

We now check the full bounded stages. They are \(K\)-stable: an integral matrix and its inverse preserve both bounding lattices in the faithful representation used in Lesson 2. By its Lemma 7.10 only finitely many dominant Cartan coweights occur in \(Y_N\). A Cartan orbit which occurs is wholly contained there, and so is its reduced closure, because \(Y_N\) is a closed stage. Their finite union, with reduced structure, equals \(Y_{N,\mathrm{red}}\). Indeed their geometric points cover it by Cartan decomposition, over every algebraically closed extension of \(k\). Equality of closed supports in a common bounded stage gives equality of their radical ideals, hence equality of the reduced closed subschemes. In the component \(\kappa\), choose \(j\) dominating all these finitely many coweights. The closure theorem gives the required inclusion into \(Z_j\). Conversely every finite-type Schubert variety has a finite lattice bound, as in the projectivity proof of Lesson 4, and therefore lies in a full \(Y_N\).

This is an exhaustion statement about the reductions. For the actual cohomology comparison let \(i:Y_{\mathrm{red}}\to Y\) be any of the closed reductions. Its open complement is empty. The closed–open triangle proved in Lesson 5, Appendices O–P, is therefore the actual unit isomorphism \[ E_Y\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }i_*i^*E_Y=i_*E_{Y_{\mathrm{red}}}. \] Apply equivariant global sections. This proves (C.12.4). The map is the pullback of constants, so it preserves the unit, cup product, and pullbacks along stage inclusions. The reduction is \(T\)-stable: a product of a reduced \(k\)-scheme with the smooth split torus is reduced, so its action factors through the reduction. The same closed–open argument applies on each finite Borel approximation and is compatible with those approximations.

No nilpotent Grassmannian family has been removed from the moduli construction. For example, \(1+\epsilon t^{-1}\) in the torus chart of C.11.1 does not factor through its reduced point. The comparison just proved identifies constant cohomology through an actual closed restriction. It does not identify the two functors of points. ∎

C.12.3. Surjective restrictions and the inverse limit

Theorem C.12.3.1. Let \(X\) be a proper closed finite union of Iwahori cells in \(\operatorname{Gr}_G\), with reduced structure, or a \(T\)-stable closed nilpotent thickening with that reduction. Then the actual restriction of graded rings \[ H_T^*(\operatorname{Gr}_G,E)\longrightarrow H_T^*(X,E) \tag{C.12.5} \] is onto. In a single component, each fixed cohomological degree stabilizes along the sequence of Lemma C.12.2.1 and is finite dimensional over \(E\). No stabilization across all components is asserted.

Proof. First let \(X\subset Y\) be two projective reduced finite cell unions, and set \(U=Y\setminus X\). Their cells admit filtrations by closed unions with one affine cell at each step. To see this, the closure of an \(I\)-orbit is \(I\)-stable and its boundary has strictly smaller dimension. It is a union of the finitely many cells of \(Y\), by the refined Cartan decomposition. Put all boundary cells before a cell in the order. At each step the new union is the preceding closed union together with that cell's closure, so is closed. Since \(X\) is already a closed cell union, its cells may be put first. The remaining order gives a closed-cell filtration of \(U\).

By Lemma C.12.1.1, a cell of dimension \(d\) is a \(T\)-representation of dimension \(d\). Its finite Borel approximation is a rank-\(d\) vector bundle \(v:V\to B_m\). Iterate the actual affine-line trace of Lesson 5, Lemma Q.1.2.1, on a trivializing open to obtain \(Rv_!E_V=E_{B_m}(-d)[-2d]\). The comparison is the smooth trace, with its given normalization, so these local comparisons agree under the vector-bundle transition maps. Equivalently the trace cone vanishes on every trivializing open and hence vanishes globally. Passing to the compatible finite Borel approximations gives the compact cell cohomology \[ H_{T,c}^q(C,E)=S^{q-2d}(-d). \tag{C.12.6} \] Here \(S^a=0\) for \(a<0\) or \(a\) odd. Applying the compact closed–open triangle successively to the filtrations proves that the compact cohomology of \(U,X,Y\) is even and finite free over \(S\). More explicitly, the odd groups vanish by induction; the resulting exact sequence in even degrees has free cell quotient, and lifting its homogeneous basis splits it as a graded \(S\)-module. On \(X,Y\), properness identifies compact and ordinary cohomology.

Now use the compact triangle for \(U\subset Y\) with closed complement \(X\). Its boundary from \(H_T^q(X)\) goes to \(H_{T,c}^{q+1}(U)\). For even \(q\) the latter vanishes, and for odd \(q\) all ordinary groups vanish. We obtain the actual graded exact sequence \[ 0\longrightarrow H_{T,c}^*(U,E) \longrightarrow H_T^*(Y,E) \longrightarrow H_T^*(X,E) \longrightarrow0. \tag{C.12.7} \] In particular restriction is onto. It is the pullback of constants and therefore is a ring map; a chosen module splitting is not being used as its definition.

Fix a component and a degree bound \(q\ge0\). By Lemma C.12.1.1 there are only finitely many cells in that component of dimension at most \(q/2\). The exhaustion in Lemma C.12.2.1 puts all of them in some \(Z_{j_0}\). For \(j'\ge j\ge j_0\), every cell in \(Z_{j'}\setminus Z_j\) has \(2d>q\). Formula (C.12.6) and its filtration show that this complement's compact cohomology vanishes in degrees at most \(q\). Sequence (C.12.7) consequently makes restriction an isomorphism in all those degrees. Each stabilized group is finite dimensional over \(E\), since a finite free graded \(S\)-module with finitely many homogeneous generators has finite dimensional homogeneous pieces.

For completeness, compute the derived compatible-section complex, so an inverse-limit term is not omitted. Write \(C_j=R\Gamma_T(Z_j,E)\) and \(f_j:C_{j+1}\to C_j\) for restriction. Derived compatible sections on this tower are the homotopy fibre \[ R\!\varprojlim_j C_j = \operatorname{fib}\left( \prod_{j\ge0}C_j \xrightarrow{\,1-\mathrm{shift}\,} \prod_{j\ge0}C_j\right), \quad (a_j)\longmapsto(a_j-f_j(a_{j+1})). \tag{C.12.8} \] Formula (C.12.8) specifies the derived compatible-section complex used here, with its canonical maps to the stages. It is invariant under componentwise quasi-isomorphisms: products of \(E\)-vector spaces are exact, so a product of quasi-isomorphisms is a quasi-isomorphism; the two products and their fibre consequently preserve such a replacement. Cohomology of the products is the product of cohomology. The fibre's long exact sequence therefore gives \[ 0\longrightarrow \operatorname{coker}\!\left(1-\mathrm{shift}: \prod_j H_T^{q-1}(Z_j)\to\prod_j H_T^{q-1}(Z_j)\right) \longrightarrow H^q(R\!\varprojlim_j C_j) \longrightarrow\varprojlim_j H_T^q(Z_j) \longrightarrow0. \tag{C.12.9} \] The cokernel is zero. Indeed all the cohomological restriction maps are onto by (C.12.7). For a prescribed sequence \((b_j)\), choose \(a_0=0\) and successively choose \(a_{j+1}\) with \(f_j(a_{j+1})=a_j-b_j\). This solves \((1-\mathrm{shift})a=b\) in each homogeneous degree. Thus the canonical map in (C.12.9) identifies cohomology with the ordinary inverse limit.

Apply the same calculation directly to the full bounded presentation. By C.12.2.1 its reduction maps are compatible quasi-isomorphisms; its reduced stages are finite Schubert unions. The preceding cell argument and (C.12.7) show that their cohomological transitions are onto. Thus (C.12.9) also identifies cohomology of derived compatible sections on the full \(Y_{N,\kappa}\) with the ordinary limit of their graded cohomology. These ordinary limits agree canonically with the limit over \(Z_j\): for each stage in either system choose a containing stage in the other and restrict a compatible family. Independence of this choice follows by restricting from a common larger stage; the two resulting operations are inverse by the same argument. This proves the comparison on cohomology without assuming a separate derived cofinality theorem.

Cup products are compatible at every stage, and the canonical projection of derived compatible sections to a stage preserves them. One may define their product on a sheaf-cochain model before taking compatible sections; compatibility says exactly that restricting a product restricts both factors. The canonical map in (C.12.9) therefore identifies this product with the product of compatible homogeneous classes, not merely with an abstract isomorphic algebra.

If a finite \(X\) lies in \(Z_m\), choose a lift of a homogeneous class in \(H_T^q(X)\) to \(H_T^q(Z_m)\) using (C.12.7). Choose successive lifts to \(Z_{m+1},Z_{m+2},\ldots\) by the same surjectivity, and define the earlier classes by restriction. This is a compatible homogeneous class, and (C.12.9) realizes it in \(H_T^q(\operatorname{Gr}_\kappa)\). This proves (C.12.5) in one component.

For the full Grassmannian, the component classification gives a disjoint union indexed by \(\Pi\). Compatible sections give a degreewise product \[ H_T^q(\operatorname{Gr}_G,E) =\prod_{\kappa\in\Pi}H_T^q(\operatorname{Gr}_\kappa,E). \tag{C.12.10} \] One can verify this on the bounded presentation: each stage meets only finitely many classes by Lesson 2, Lemma 7.10, and enlarging stages covers every class. Thus a compatible family is exactly a family of compatible classes on all components. A finite \(X\) meets finitely many components. Lift its class in each of them as above and use zero in the others. This proves the global surjectivity, even when the centre contributes infinitely many classes.

Finally, for a nilpotent thickening \(X\) the actual closed restriction to \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\) is the multiplicative isomorphism proved in C.12.2.1. Its composite with (C.12.5) is the already proved surjection onto that reduction, so restriction onto \(X\) is onto as well. ∎

Corollary C.12.3.2. For complexes \(P,Q\) supported on such an \(X\), the actual whole-Grassmannian action gives \[ \operatorname{Hom}_{H_T^*(\operatorname{Gr}_G)}^* \bigl(H_T^*(X,P),H_T^*(X,Q)\bigr) = \operatorname{Hom}_{H_T^*(X)}^* \bigl(H_T^*(X,P),H_T^*(X,Q)\bigr). \tag{C.12.11} \] Whenever the hypotheses of C.10 hold on \(X\), its comparison theorem therefore holds with the whole Grassmannian cohomology ring.

Proof. Both module actions factor through actual restriction to \(H_T^*(X)\): cup product with a supported complex first restricts the constant class to its support. If a homogeneous map commutes with the whole-Grassmannian action, lift any homogeneous class of \(H_T^*(X)\) using C.12.3.1. Commutation with that lift is commutation with the given class. Conversely commutation with every support class implies commutation with their restrictions. These are the same maps, with the same degrees and compositions. The last assertion is now exactly C.10 applied to this equality; no stalk-surjectivity hypothesis has been supplied by the corollary. ∎

C.12.4. Two exercises on the component qualification

Exercise C.12.4.1 (basic). Compute the ring in (C.12.10) for \(G=\mathbb G_m\). Explain why the full Grassmannian does not have finite dimensional degree-zero cohomology, despite degreewise stabilization on each component.

Solution. Here \(\Lambda=\Pi=\mathbb Z\), \(Q^\vee=0\), and each reduced component is one point \(t^n\). Its full stages may retain the nilpotents displayed in C.11.1, but C.12.2.1 identifies their constant cohomology with that point's cohomology. With \(u=c_1(\chi)\), \[ H_T^*(\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb G_m},E) =\prod_{n\in\mathbb Z}^{\mathrm{degreewise}}E[u]. \tag{C.12.12} \] The superscript specifies the graded ring: in each degree one takes the product of that degree's vector spaces, with componentwise multiplication. It is not a direct sum of component rings. In degree zero it is \(\prod_{n\in\mathbb Z}E\), which is infinite dimensional. Restriction to finitely many components is the product projection and is onto. Each individual component is already stable at its reduced point, in every degree.

Exercise C.12.4.2 (intermediate). For \(G=PGL_2\), choose the coweight coordinate \(n\in\mathbb Z\) with \(\langle\alpha,n\rangle=n\). List the dimensions of the cells \(n=-2,-1,0,1,2\) and the classes in which they lie. Why does dimension zero not identify the two components?

Solution. The coroot is \(2\), so \(\Pi=\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z\). Formula (C.12.1) is \[ d_I(n)=\max(0,n)+\max(0,-n-1). \] The listed dimensions are \(1,0,0,1,2\), respectively; their classes are \(0,1,0,1,0\). Thus both components contain a zero-dimensional cell. In the odd component the point \(n=-1\) is the closed cell of the minimal orbit \(\mathbf P^1\), while its other cell, at \(n=1\), has dimension one. In the even component \(n=0\) is the base point. The locally constant coroot class, not dimension, separates them. Within either class, the inequality \(|n|\le d_I(n)+1\) bounds the coweight labels of cells of bounded dimension.

C.13. Finite jets, constant-group restriction and actual mapping complexes

The earlier proof inputs are Lesson 1, Theorem 2.C, Lesson 2, Theorem 7.1, and Lesson 5, Lemmas Q.1.1.1-Q.1.2.1. The finite-jet generator enhancement is Theorem A.4.1.1 above. The full quotient schemes and the actual coefficient and mapping-complex comparisons are proved here.

Let \(H_m\) be the algebraic group with \[ H_m(R)=G(R[t]/t^{m+1}),\qquad m\ge0, \] so \(H_0=G\). Constant loops give a section \(G\to H_m\) of evaluation. We work on a finite \(G(k[[t]])\)-stable closed support \(X\) contained in a full bounded stage \(Y_N\). The quotient-stack notation below denotes the actual action-nerve constructible category, or its finite free-frame models, with the maps of those presentations. The enhancements and coefficient comparisons used in A.4 are retained.

C.13.1. The whole finite-jet quotient

Lemma C.13.1.1. Evaluation has kernel \[ U_m=\ker(H_m\longrightarrow G)\simeq\mathbf A^{m\dim G} \tag{C.13.1} \] as a scheme, over every parameter algebra. Multiplication identifies \(H_m=U_m\rtimes G\), and right quotient by the constant group identifies \(H_m/G\) with the actual scheme \(U_m\). More generally, \[ U_{n,m}=\ker(H_n\longrightarrow H_m) \simeq\mathbf A^{(n-m)\dim G}\quad(n\ge m). \tag{C.13.2} \] The arc action on \(Y_N\), and hence on \(X\), factors through \(H_m\) whenever \(m+1\ge2N\).

Proof. The ordered big cell of Lesson 1, Theorem 2.C is a principal open containing the identity, with coordinates \(U^-TU^+\). Any finite jet equal to the identity modulo \(t\) lies in this open: its defining function has an invertible constant term, hence is invertible modulo \(t^{m+1}\), on every algebra \(R\). Its negative and positive root coordinates are uniquely \(\sum_{j=1}^m a_{\alpha,j}t^j\). Choose a character basis for the original split torus. Each of its coordinates is uniquely \[ 1+\sum_{j=1}^m b_{i,j}t^j. \] These expressions are units; their inverses are the finite geometric series in the nilpotent ideal \((t)\). Every displayed coefficient is arbitrary in \(R\). There are \(m(|\Phi|+\operatorname{rank}T)=m\dim G\) coefficients. The big-cell multiplication and inverse coordinate maps give (C.13.1) on arbitrary test algebras. This includes every central torus coordinate.

For the kernel in (C.13.2), impose equality to the identity modulo \(t^{m+1}\) instead. Uniqueness of the same big-cell coordinates sets precisely their coefficients of degrees at most \(m\) to zero; their coefficients of degrees \(m+1,\ldots,n\) are arbitrary. This proves (C.13.2), retaining the full kernel schemes.

For \(h\in H_m(R)\), let \(g_0=h(0)\). The unique expression \(h=u g_0\), with \(u=h g_0^{-1}\in U_m(R)\), proves the semidirect product. Under right multiplication by \(a\in G(R)\), the expression \(h h(0)^{-1}\) is unchanged, because \((h a)(h(0)a)^{-1}=h h(0)^{-1}\). Thus it gives the quotient map and its section \(u\mapsto u\), identifying the whole right quotient with \(U_m\).

The map \(G(R[[t]])\to H_m(R)\) is onto. After dividing a given jet by its constant value, its big-cell coordinates have the displayed form. Extend the root polynomials to formal series and the torus coordinates to the same units in \(R[[t]]\). Their product, times the constant value, is a lift. This argument also proves surjectivity \(H_n(R)\to H_m(R)\), by using the same expressions modulo \(t^{n+1}\).

Finally take the faithful matrix embedding and the full lattice bounds from Lesson 2. A matrix \(h=1+t^{m+1}A(t)\), and its inverse, have this congruence form in that embedding. For every bounded lattice \(L\) and every \(\ell\in L\), \[ h\ell-\ell\in t^{m+1-N}R[[t]]^{\dim V} \subset t^N R[[t]]^{\dim V}\subset L \qquad(m+1\ge2N). \] Thus \(hL\subset L\), and the inverse inclusion follows from \(h^{-1}\). The associated-lattice closed immersion of the full Grassmannian in Lesson 2 identifies equality of these lattices with equality of the Grassmannian points, on every algebra. The entire congruence kernel therefore acts as identity on the full \(Y_N\), including its nilpotent parameters. Surjectivity onto \(H_m\) makes the action factor through that finite jet group. ∎

C.13.2. Restriction to the constant group

Theorem C.13.2.1. For an \(H_m\)-action on \(X\), let \[ p:[X/G]\longrightarrow[X/H_m] \] be induced by inclusion of the constant group. For every constructible coefficient complex \(F\) on the target, its actual ordinary unit is an isomorphism \[ F\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }Rp_*p^*F. \tag{C.13.3} \] Consequently restriction gives canonical isomorphisms \[ \begin{gathered} R\Gamma_{H_m}(X,F)\xrightarrow{\sim}R\Gamma_G(X,p^*F),\\ R\operatorname{Hom}_{H_m}(P,Q) \xrightarrow{\sim}R\operatorname{Hom}_G(p^*P,p^*Q). \end{gathered} \tag{C.13.4} \] They preserve units, cup actions, shifts, composition and, for Weil objects, the actual Frobenius operators.

Proof. Pull \(p\) back along the smooth action atlas \(X\to[X/H_m]\). The resulting map is \[ X\times(H_m/G)\longrightarrow X. \] Indeed an isomorphism between two trivial \(H_m\)-torsors is an element \(h\); the equality of their maps to \(X\) determines the second point as \(h^{-1}x\), and its change by a constant \(G\)-element divides \(h\) on the right. Lemma C.13.1.1 identifies this quotient with \(U_m\), an affine space. Thus the pulled-back map is an actual product affine-space projection, and the pulled coefficient is the pullback of \(F|_X\).

Iterate the ordinary affine-line unit of Lesson 5, Lemma Q.1.2.1. Its \(m\dim G\) iterations show that the ordinary unit on this product projection is an isomorphism, for every such pulled coefficient. Ordinary smooth base change, with its actual counit-transpose comparison in Lesson 5, Lemma Q.1.1.1, identifies this map with the pullback of (C.13.3). Smooth descent is conservative on the action presentation: if a cone restricts to zero on its atlas, every level of its cartesian nerve diagram is zero, so the cone is zero. This proves (C.13.3); no nonproper arbitrary-fibre comparison is used.

Apply global sections to its actual unit. Composition of ordinary images identifies the target with \(R\Gamma_G(X,p^*F)\), giving the first map of (C.13.4). For the second, derived adjunction gives \[ R\operatorname{Hom}_G(p^*P,p^*Q) = R\operatorname{Hom}_{H_m}(P,Rp_*p^*Q). \] Under this equality the restriction map is precisely the map induced by the unit of \(Q\). Formula (C.13.3) makes it an isomorphism. This argument needs no asserted internal-Hom pullback formula for singular constructible complexes.

Restriction is a functor on the actual coefficient presentations, so it preserves identities, shifts and composition. Pullback preserves multiplication of constant cochains and their action on pulled coefficients, giving the cup and coefficient-ring compatibility. Every map used is an actual unit, pullback, ordinary-image comparison or adjunction, defined over the ground field. If the objects have Weil structures, naturality therefore intertwines those structures and the resulting Frobenius operators. A choice of affine coordinates was used to test the unit, not to define it. ∎

C.13.3. Increasing the jet level

Lemma C.13.3.1. Suppose the action on \(X\) factors through \(H_m\), and let \(n\ge m\). For the quotient-stack map \(q:[X/H_n]\to[X/H_m]\), the actual unit is an isomorphism \[ F\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }Rq_*q^*F. \tag{C.13.5} \] Thus increasing a sufficient jet level preserves all mapping complexes between objects defined at that level. In the finite-jet spherical generator enhancement of A.4, the restriction maps in C.13.2 consequently identify the actual \(G(k[[t]])\)-equivariant generator mapping complexes with their constant-\(G\) restrictions.

Proof. The kernel \(V=U_{n,m}\) acts trivially on \(X\). Pulling \(q\) back along the action atlas gives \(X\times BV\to X\): a lift of a trivial \(H_m\)-torsor exists as the trivial \(H_n\)-torsor, and its automorphisms over that torsor are exactly \(V\). This describes the entire quotient stack, retaining all its automorphisms.

Every level \(V^s\) of the group nerve is an affine space, by C.13.1.1. For the coefficient pulled from \(X\), the actual ordinary affine-line units give \[ F|_X\xrightarrow{\sim} R(\operatorname{pr}_X:X\times V^s\to X)_* \operatorname{pr}_X^*(F|_X). \] These units commute with faces and degeneracies, since all of those maps preserve the projection to \(X\). The augmented cosimplicial complex of the constant diagrams on the left has identity face and degeneracy maps. Its normalized complex is \(F|_X\) in degree zero and zero in positive simplicial degrees: in degree \(s>0\) a normalized element is in the kernel of a degeneracy which is the identity. The same computation can be made in any bounded coefficient-resolution range and then in the full bounded-below total complex; the affine-space units are quasi-isomorphisms at each level, and the first-quadrant degree filtration makes them a quasi-isomorphism on totalizations. It follows that the ordinary image of the pulled coefficient on \(X\times BV\) is exactly \(F|_X\), with the displayed unit.

Descent on the action atlas, or on the compatible free-frame diagrams of A.1, identifies this comparison with (C.13.5). To spell out the ordinary-image calculation, inverse image of the target atlas computes the fibre groupoid just described; its cartesian nerve has the projections \(X\times V^s\to X\). Ordinary image on that presentation is their cosimplicial totalization. Thus the comparison is a calculation on the actual presentation, not an inference from the cohomology of individual geometric fibres. Conservativity of that presentation proves (C.13.5).

Global sections and the same derived-adjunction argument as in C.13.2 give cohomology and mapping-complex isomorphisms. The composition \(G\to H_n\to H_m\) is the original constant inclusion. Hence these isomorphisms commute with the constant-group restrictions. The generator enhancement of A.4 is formed from these action and coefficient presentations at sufficient jet levels; the units just proved identify its mapping complexes at every larger level with those at the chosen finite level, and then with (C.13.4). All identifications are the actual restriction functors and retain their compositions and Frobenius structures. This proves the stated generator comparison. It does not assert that every \(G\)-equivariant constructible object on \(X\) comes from an \(H_m\)-equivariant object. ∎

C.13.4. Two finite-jet exercises

Exercise C.13.4.1 (basic). Compute \(U_2\) for \(G=\mathbb G_m\), including its multiplication, over an arbitrary parameter algebra. Explain why its trivial constant cohomology does not mean it is the trivial group scheme.

Solution. Its units are \(1+at+bt^2\) modulo \(t^3\). Multiplication is \[ (a,b)(a',b')=(a+a',\,b+b'+aa'), \qquad (a,b)^{-1}=(-a,\,a^2-b). \tag{C.13.6} \] Thus the scheme is \(\mathbf A^2\), with a genuine group law in every characteristic. Its ordinary constant cohomology is \(E\) by two affine-line units; the relative nerve calculation in C.13.3 makes \(R\Gamma(BU_2,E)=E\). Neither comparison identifies its points or its group law with those of the trivial group. In particular all \(a,b\), including nilpotent parameters, remain in the quotient scheme \(H_2/G\).

Exercise C.13.4.2 (intermediate). For \(G=GL_2\), describe \(U_1\) and determine a sufficient jet level for action on a lattice stage with bounds \(t^N O^2\subset L\subset t^{-N}O^2\).

Solution. Every point of \(U_1\) is uniquely \(1+tA\) modulo \(t^2\), with \(A\in M_2(R)\); its inverse is \(1-tA\), and the group law is addition of \(A\). Hence \(U_1\) is the four-dimensional additive matrix scheme. For the bounded lattice stage, a congruence \(h=1+t^{m+1}A(t)\) changes any lattice vector by an element of \(t^{m+1-N}O^2\). This is inside \(t^NO^2\subset L\) when \(m+1\ge2N\), and the inverse congruence gives equality of lattices. Therefore \(H_m\) acts for \(m\ge\max(0,2N-1)\). This is a sufficient uniform bound; it is not claimed to be the minimum for each individual lattice.

C.14. The flag basis and actual torus-to-group comparison

We use the proved invariant-degree identities of Lemma B.1.1.1 above, the actual Euler and trace comparisons of C.5 and Lesson 6 A.1, and the finite-jet comparisons of C.13. The additional commutative-algebra and flag-coordinate proofs are linked at their point of use. Every change-of-group map below is restriction followed by the specified cup action.

Keep the original split connected reductive \(G/k\), with \(k\) algebraically closed, and \(E/\mathbb Q_\ell\) finite, with \(\ell\) invertible in \(k\). Write \[ S=\operatorname{Sym}_E(X^*(T)\otimes_{\mathbb Z}E), \qquad R=S^W,\qquad N=|\Phi^+|. \] A character has polynomial degree one and cohomological degree two. Its Chern class retains its coefficient twist \(E(1)\). Thus a homogeneous class of polynomial degree \(a\) gives the action \(F(-a)[-2a]\to F\). We suppress those lines only when writing graded coefficient rings.

C.14.1. The finite coinvariant algebra

Lemma C.14.1.1. Choose homogeneous polynomial generators \(f_1,\ldots,f_r\) of \(R\), of polynomial degrees \(d_i\), as in B.1.1.1. Put \(C=S/(f_1,\ldots,f_r)\). Then \[ \dim_E C=|W|,\qquad C^{>N}=0,\qquad \operatorname{Soc}(C)=C^N\simeq E, \quad N=\sum_i(d_i-1). \tag{C.14.1} \] Every nonzero ideal of \(C\) meets this socle. If \(b_1,\ldots,b_{|W|}\) are homogeneous lifts of an \(E\)-basis of \(C\), then they form an \(R\)-basis of \(S\). They may be chosen with \(b_1=1\).

Proof. B.1.1.1 proves polynomiality, the sum of the degrees, and \(\prod_i d_i=|W|\), for this Weyl action including its central fixed space. The polynomial ring \(S\) is finite over \(R\): each coordinate \(x_j\) satisfies the monic orbit polynomial \(\prod_{w\in W}(Z-wx_j)\), whose coefficients are invariant. Reducing powers by these finitely many monic equations expresses \(S\) as a finite \(R\)-module. Hence \(C\) is finite dimensional. Its positive ideal \(\mathfrak n_C\) is nilpotent by its finite positive grading, and \(C/\mathfrak n_C=E\); it is therefore local.

The list \(f_1,\ldots,f_r\) is a system of parameters in \(S_{(x_1,\ldots,x_r)}\). A polynomial ring over a field is Cohen–Macaulay by Regular sequences, depth and Cohen–Macaulay modules, Theorem 5.3. Its Corollary 4.3 proves that this parameter list is regular. It is regular already in the graded polynomial ring: a nonzero homogeneous element of a successive homogeneous quotient stays nonzero on localizing at \((x_1,\ldots,x_r)\). Indeed multiplication by a polynomial with nonzero constant term has nonzero least-degree component on such an element. A homogeneous annihilator in a kernel would consequently contradict local regularity.

The successive multiplication sequences for this regular list give \[ \operatorname{Hilb}_C(z) =\frac{\prod_i(1-z^{d_i})}{(1-z)^r} =\prod_i(1+z+\cdots+z^{d_i-1}). \tag{C.14.2} \] Its degree is \(N\), its top coefficient is one, and its value at one is \(|W|\).

We also prove that no smaller-degree socle is hidden. The Koszul complex on a regular sequence is a free resolution of its quotient: start with the two-term injective multiplication complex, and tensor the next such complex; its cone has cohomology only the next quotient because the next multiplication is injective. This induction applies to both the coordinate sequence \(x_1,\ldots,x_r\) resolving \(E\), and \(f_1,\ldots,f_r\) resolving \(C\).

Compute their balanced \(\operatorname{Tor}_r^S(E,C)\) using these two resolutions. Equality of the computations follows by taking their finite double tensor complex: resolve one factor first by rows and then the other by columns, using the just proved acyclicity of their free resolutions. In the coordinate Koszul complex tensored with \(C\), its top cycles are precisely the elements of \(\operatorname{Soc}(C)\), with their polynomial degrees increased by \(r\); there is no incoming top boundary. In the \(f\)-Koszul complex tensored with \(E\), every differential is zero because every \(f_i\) has positive degree. Its top term is one line of polynomial degree \(\sum_i d_i=N+r\). Thus the socle is exactly one line in degree \(N\), proving (C.14.1).

Every nonzero ideal meets it. Given a nonzero element in that ideal, take the largest power of the nilpotent maximal ideal whose product with the generated ideal is nonzero. Any nonzero element of that product is killed by the maximal ideal and belongs to the socle.

Finally the lifts \(b_j\) generate \(S\) over \(R\) by induction on polynomial degree: reduce an element modulo \((f_i)\) in the chosen basis, and express the remainder as \(\sum_i f_i a_i\), with each \(a_i\) of smaller degree. The free graded \(R\)-module on these lifts has Hilbert series \(\operatorname{Hilb}_R(z)\operatorname{Hilb}_C(z)=\operatorname{Hilb}_S(z)\), by (C.14.2). The resulting surjection is a bijection in each finite dimensional homogeneous degree. It is therefore the required free basis. The unique degree-zero basis class is one. All these arguments include the torus case, where the \(d_i\) are one and \(C=E\). ∎

C.14.2. Chern classes on the flag variety

Let \(F=G/B\). Use the associated line convention \[ \mathcal L_\chi=G\times^B k_\chi,\qquad (g,z)\cdot b=(gb,\chi(b)^{-1}z). \] At \(n_wB\) its \(T\)-character is \(w\chi\). The root-coordinate Bruhat proof in Root data and Weyl groups, Theorems 6.2 and 8.1, and the smooth projective quotient of The flag scheme and the Weyl group, Theorem 1.1, give a closed affine paving of \(F\) indexed by \(W\), and the \(T\)-fixed points \(n_wB\). Its tangent characters there are \(-w\alpha\), for \(\alpha>0\). Put \(\delta=\prod_{\alpha>0}(-\alpha)\in S^N\).

Theorem C.14.2.1. The actual Chern map gives an isomorphism \[ C\xrightarrow{\sim}H^*(F,E),\qquad [\chi]\longmapsto c_1(\mathcal L_\chi). \tag{C.14.3} \] The class \(c(\delta)\) has trace \(|W|\), with coefficient line \(E(N)\). In particular the images \(c(b_j)\) of C.14.1 form a homogeneous basis of flag cohomology.

Proof. Bruhat root coordinates are linear for the constant torus and have nonzero weights. The closed-cell trace induction of C.12.3 proves that \(H_T^*(F,E)\) is finite free over \(S\), in even degrees. The same induction without the torus proves that ordinary flag cohomology has one generator for each cell, in its compact degree \(2d\), with twist \(-d\).

The actual forgetful restriction gives \[ H_T^*(F,E)\otimes_S E\xrightarrow{\sim}H^*(F,E). \tag{C.14.4} \] Here is the comparison, rather than a general assertion that base change commutes with Hom. At each finite torus model choose a point of \(B_m\) with a frame; pulling back gives \(F\). On each cell the compact trace comparison is \(S(-d)[-2d]\to E(-d)[-2d]\), so is exactly augmentation of its coefficient ring. The compact cell rows are short exact with free cell quotient, both equivariantly and ordinarily. Tensoring the former with \(E\) remains exact because that quotient is free. Induction in the comparison of these rows proves (C.14.4), with its actual pullback. It also proves multiplicativity, since pullback of constants preserves cup product.

For any homogeneous \(f\in R\), the equivariant Chern class \(c_T(f)\) restricts at \(n_wB\) to \(w(f)=f\). The base class \(f\in S\) has those same restrictions. C.5.2.1 says that their difference is zero after localization. Freeness over the polynomial domain \(S\) says that a class killed by a nonzero denominator is zero already. Thus \[ c_T(f)=\pi^*f. \tag{C.14.5} \] When \(f\) has positive degree, (C.14.4) sends the right side to zero. This proves that the ordinary Chern map factors through \(C\).

Compute the trace of \(c(\delta)\) with its actual orientation. At a fixed point \(p=n_wB\), a translated root chart is an affine neighbourhood with coordinate tangent characters \(-w\alpha\). Compose its coordinate zero-section comparisons from Lesson 6, A.1.1.1–A.1.2.1. The supported point class has restriction \[ e_p=\prod_{\alpha>0}(-w\alpha)=w\delta \] at \(p\), and zero restriction at the other fixed points. Its structural trace is one by composition of the section and structural trace counits. After inverting \(S\setminus0\), C.5.2.1 therefore expresses any equivariant class \(a\) as the sum of these supported point classes with coefficients \(a|_p/e_p\). Applying trace gives \[ \int_F a=\sum_{w\in W}\frac{a|_{n_wB}}{w\delta}. \tag{C.14.6} \] This identity is in the localized coefficient ring and retains the twists; the supported classes, self-intersections and trace defined it. For \(a=c_T(\delta)\), every fraction is one. Its degree-normalized trace is consequently \(|W|\). The trace is constant in degree zero, so its localized equality is its integral equality. Forgetful pullback and trace compatibility give the same number for \(c(\delta)\). It is nonzero in \(E\), regardless of the geometric characteristic.

Thus \([\delta]\) is nonzero in \(C^N\), and generates its socle by C.14.1. If the Chern map had a nonzero kernel ideal, that ideal would meet this socle and would kill \(c(\delta)\), which has nonzero trace. Its kernel is therefore zero. Both sides of (C.14.3) have dimension \(|W|\), the right side by its closed affine paving. The map is onto as well. ∎

C.14.3. Relative flag decomposition and restriction

For a finite-type \(G\)-scheme \(X\) and actual equivariant bounded constructible complexes, write \(\operatorname{res}\) for restriction of the action presentation to \(T\). Cohomology of quotient stacks is computed in fixed ranges by the actual free frames of A.1. Write \(a_j=\deg b_j\).

Theorem C.14.3.1. Actual restriction identifies \(H_G^*(\mathrm{pt},E)\) with \(R=S^W\). For every \(G\)-equivariant \(F,P,Q\) on \(X\), the maps \[ \begin{gathered} H_G^*(X,F)\otimes_R S \xrightarrow{\sim}H_T^*(X,\operatorname{res}F),\\ \operatorname{Ext}_G^*(P,Q)\otimes_R S \xrightarrow{\sim} \operatorname{Ext}_T^*(\operatorname{res}P,\operatorname{res}Q) \end{gathered} \tag{C.14.7} \] send \(x\otimes s\) to \(s\cup\operatorname{res}(x)\). They retain the shifts and coefficient twists specified above. The Ext comparison preserves identities and Yoneda composition under this scalar extension.

Proof. First compare \(T\subset B\). Ordered root coordinates identify \(B/T\) with the full affine scheme of the unipotent radical. On a smooth action atlas, the map \([X/T]\to[X/B]\) is the product projection with this fibre. The iterated ordinary affine-line unit and Lemma Q.1.1.1, followed by conservative descent on the atlas, give the actual unit isomorphism, exactly as in C.13.2. This proves the corresponding cohomology and mapping-complex comparisons by adjunction. It keeps the action presentations, rather than replacing them by an arbitrary fibre calculation.

Now consider the proper smooth flag map \[ p:[X/B]\longrightarrow[X/G]. \] Its pullback to the action atlas is \(X\times F\to X\). On \([X/B]\) the character lines from \(BB\) give the actual classes \(c(b_j)\). Compose the adjunction unit of a target coefficient \(K\) with cup action by these classes to define \[ \Phi_K: \bigoplus_j K(-a_j)[-2a_j]\longrightarrow Rp_*p^*K. \tag{C.14.8} \] On the atlas, proper base change and projection identify the target with \(K|_X\otimes R\Gamma(F,E)\). The pulled classes are the flag classes of C.14.2.1, so their basis makes this displayed map an isomorphism. One can verify the product coefficient identity by the same compact-cell trace filtration used in C.14.4; its arrows are the proper pullback and projection comparisons. Conservativity on the action atlas proves (C.14.8) globally. In particular this decomposition concerns the actual unit and global character classes, not only the fibre dimensions.

Apply global sections for cohomology. For mapping complexes use derived adjunction: \[ R\operatorname{Hom}_B(p^*P,p^*Q) =R\operatorname{Hom}_G(P,Rp_*p^*Q). \] Inserting (C.14.8) gives the direct sum of the shifted and twisted \(G\)-mapping complexes. Its \(j\)-th map is cup action by \(b_j\) after actual restriction. No internal-Hom pullback formula is needed.

It remains to identify the coefficient ring used in (C.14.7). Apply this decomposition with \(X=\mathrm{pt}\). Since \(b_1=1\), the actual map \(H_G^*(\mathrm{pt})\to S\) is injective: it is the first summand of the resulting basis decomposition. Its image lies in \(S^W\). Indeed, on a common free \(G\)-frame the right action of \(N_G(T)\) on the quotient by \(T\) preserves its projection to the quotient by \(G\); it factors through \(W\) and transforms character lines by the Weyl action. Pullback from the quotient by \(G\) is invariant under these actual maps.

The flag decomposition and (C.14.2) give, degree by degree, \[ \operatorname{Hilb}_{H_G^*(\mathrm{pt})}(z) =\frac{\operatorname{Hilb}_{S}(z)} {\sum_j z^{a_j}} =\prod_i(1-z^{d_i})^{-1} =\operatorname{Hilb}_{R}(z). \tag{C.14.9} \] There are no odd groups: a degree-zero basis summand would otherwise produce an odd group in \(S\), which has none. The same summand proves finiteness of every degree. Thus the invariant inclusion has equal dimensions in each degree and is onto \(R\). It is a ring map, so identifies the coefficient rings multiplicatively.

Lemma C.14.1.1 makes \(S=\bigoplus_j Rb_j\). Therefore the cohomology and Ext direct-sum comparisons just constructed are precisely (C.14.7). Restriction is an actual functor and cup coefficients have even degrees, so \[ (s\cup\operatorname{res}u)\circ (t\cup\operatorname{res}v) =st\cup\operatorname{res}(u\circ v). \tag{C.14.10} \] There is no odd coefficient sign. Identities map to identities. This proves the asserted composition compatibility; it does not claim a monoidal enhancement from graded Ext alone. All maps are defined over the ground field and intertwine Weil structures when supplied. ∎

C.14.4. Weyl descent of actual morphisms

Let \(A_G=H_G^*(X,E)\), \(A_T=H_T^*(X,E)\), and \(M_G=H_G^*(X,P)\), \(N_G=H_G^*(X,Q)\). The actual \(G\)-equivariances give a Weyl action on their torus restrictions: representatives in the normalizer conjugate the torus action, and the action cocycle identifies the pulled coefficients. Representatives differing by \(T\) give the same map on equivariant cohomology and Ext, by that cocycle. These are semilinear actions over \(S\).

Corollary C.14.4.1. These actions yield \[ \begin{gathered} \operatorname{Ext}_G^*(P,Q) =\operatorname{Ext}_T^*(\operatorname{res}P,\operatorname{res}Q)^W,\\ \operatorname{Hom}_{A_G}^*(M_G,N_G) =\operatorname{Hom}_{A_T}^*(M_G\otimes_R S,N_G\otimes_R S)^W. \end{gathered} \tag{C.14.11} \] The maps are actual restriction and scalar extension, and retain composition. If the comparison of C.10 holds for the torus restrictions of \(P,Q\) on \(X\), it therefore holds for their \(G\)-equivariant Ext and \(A_G\)-module Hom. For a sufficiently large finite jet action, C.13 gives the same assertion for the original spherical generator Ext.

Proof. C.14.3 identifies each torus group with scalar extension from \(R\), and identifies \(A_T=A_G\otimes_R S\) as rings: the multiplication comparison is the restriction and cup map. Under these identifications \(W\) acts trivially on the \(G\)-factor and ordinarily on \(S\), since all restricted classes came from a \(G\)-presentation.

For every \(R\)-module \(L\), the invariant part of \(L\otimes_R S\) is exactly \(L\). To prove this without a flat Hom-base-change assertion, average the \(W\)-action using \(|W|^{-1}\sum_w w\). This projector is \(R\)-linear on \(S\) with image \(R\), and is the identity on that submodule. On \(L\otimes_R S\) its image is therefore \(L\otimes_R R=L\); this inclusion is injective because the same projector splits it. Apply this first to the Ext scalar extension to get the first equation.

For an \(A_T\)-linear invariant map \(h\) in the second line, \(h(m\otimes1)\) is invariant and hence belongs to the canonically embedded \(N_G\). It defines a homogeneous map \(h_0:M_G\to N_G\). Applying the actual restriction of each \(a\in A_G\) proves \(h_0\) is \(A_G\)-linear. Every other value of \(h\) is determined by \(S\)-linearity: \(h(m\otimes s)=s\,h_0(m)\). Conversely scalar extension of an \(A_G\)-linear map gives such an invariant \(A_T\)-linear map. These inverse constructions preserve composition. They require neither projectivity of \(M_G\) nor a claim that arbitrary Hom commutes with specialization.

Finally the cohomology comparison map of C.10 is natural under these normalizer maps, because it applies actual cohomology to actual morphisms. Its Weyl invariants therefore give the comparison of the first and second lines of (C.14.11). The finite-jet and constant-group restriction of C.13 identifies the original generator Ext with this one, preserving the coefficient action and composition. The hypotheses of C.10, including its stalk-surjectivity requirement, remain required. ∎

Corollary C.14.4.2. On the full Grassmannian, constant cohomology satisfies \[ H_G^*(\operatorname{Gr}_G,E)\otimes_R S \xrightarrow{\sim}H_T^*(\operatorname{Gr}_G,E). \tag{C.14.12} \] For every finite closed \(G(k[[t]])\)-stable support \(X\) whose reduction is a union of Iwahori cells, actual restriction \(H_G^*(\operatorname{Gr}_G)\to H_G^*(X)\) is onto. The comparison of C.10, when its stalk hypotheses hold, therefore identifies the original spherical generator Ext with module Hom over the whole \(G\)-equivariant Grassmannian ring.

Proof. Use the original full bounded stages \(Y_N\); they are \(G\)-stable by the matrix-bound argument of C.12.2. C.14.3 applies at every such stage and commutes with its restriction maps. Its decomposition has the fixed finite basis \(b_j\), independent of \(N\). Finite direct sums and fixed shifts commute with the two products and their fibre in the derived compatible-section model (C.12.8). Thus the same flag decomposition holds for derived compatible sections on the full bounded presentation. The canonical cohomology map gives (C.14.12), with the same actual cup products and Weyl action.

Under this comparison \(W\) acts on the scalar factor \(S\) alone. Averaging as in C.14.4 identifies its invariants with \(H_G^*(\operatorname{Gr}_G)\), also for degreewise products over all components. The restriction in C.12.3 is a Weyl-equivariant surjection for this \(G\)-stable \(X\). Given an invariant target class, lift it by that surjection and average the lift; the averaged class is an invariant lift. Hence the \(G\)-restriction is onto. Reduction and the actual closed-restriction unit retain the assertion for the stated nilpotent support structures.

Consequently an \(H_G^*(X)\)-module map between supported cohomology modules is exactly a map linear over the whole ring: lift each homogeneous support class through the surjection, as in C.12.3.2. Combine this equality with C.14.4.1 and the actual jet comparison of C.13. The result retains composition and the original group and components. The unconditional stalk-surjectivity input, geometric identification of these modules with Appendix B, and enhanced convolution remain separate requirements. ∎

C.14.5. Two change-of-group exercises

Exercise C.14.5.1 (basic). For the rank-one Weyl action \(u\mapsto-u\) compute \(R,C\), a free \(R\)-basis of \(S\), and the invariant part of \(L\otimes_R S\) for an arbitrary \(R\)-module \(L\).

Solution. With polynomial degree \(|u|=1\), \(S=E[u]\), \(R=E[u^2]\), \(C=E[u]/(u^2)\), and \(S=R\oplus Ru\). Thus \(L\otimes_R S=L\oplus Lu\); its Weyl action is \((a,b)\mapsto(a,-b)\). Since two is invertible in \(E\), its invariants are exactly the first copy \(L\), including when \(L\) has \(u^2\)-torsion. The quotient \(C\) has its one-dimensional socle generated by \(u\). This verifies both the free basis and the averaging argument without a torsion-free hypothesis on \(L\).

Exercise C.14.5.2 (intermediate). For \(G=SL_2\), let \(\chi(\operatorname{diag}(z,z^{-1}))=z\) and \(u=c_1(\chi)\). Determine the sign of \(\mathcal L_\chi\) on \(G/B=\mathbf P^1\) in our associated-line convention, and check the tangent-root trace.

Solution. The upper Borel fixes the line spanned by \(e_1\). The map \([g,z]\mapsto z\,ge_1\) identifies \(\mathcal L_\chi\) with the tautological line: replacing \((g,z)\) by \((gb,\chi(b)^{-1}z)\) leaves that vector unchanged. Therefore \(c(u)=-h\), where \(h=c_1(\mathcal O(1))\) has trace one. The positive root is \(\alpha=2\chi\), and \(\delta=-\alpha=-2u\); hence \(c(\delta)=2h\), with trace \(2=|W|\). At the two fixed points the tangent Euler classes are \(-2u\) and \(2u\), respectively. Formula (C.14.6) for \(c_T(\delta)\) sums two fractions equal to one. This checks the sign and also retains the nonprimitive integral root character.

C.15. Relative tame boundary comparison with complete coefficients

Let \(Y_0\) be smooth and integral over a finite field of characteristic \(p\), let \(p_0:\overline X_0\to Y_0\) be a smooth proper curve, and let \(D_0\subset\overline X_0\) be a Cartier divisor finite étale over \(Y_0\). Write \(j:U_0=\overline X_0\setminus D_0\hookrightarrow\overline X_0\) and \(i:D_0\hookrightarrow\overline X_0\). Fix a finite extension \(E/\mathbb Q_\ell\), with \(\ell\ne p\), valuation ring \(O\), and uniformizer \(\pi\). Let \(L\) be a lisse \(E\)-sheaf of rank \(r\ge1\). Tameness means that the finite reductions of a stable lattice are tame at the generic boundary DVRs. All comparisons below concern the actual ordinary extension \(j_*L=\mathcal H^0Rj_*L\).

The local finite-cover input has a complete earlier proof: Specialization and tame ramification, Theorem 2.3, identifies tame covers along a regular Cartier divisor with finite Kummer covers after passage to a strict local scheme. The coefficient inputs are The pro-étale site and l-adic complexes, Theorem 7.10 and Corollary 7.11, and Semi-infinite geometry and MV weight functors, M.6.2.1 and O.2.1.1. Their completed pullback and ordinary-image proofs retain the actual coefficient comparisons. We use them rather than commuting an infinite inverse limit with an ordinary étale stalk.

C.15.1. The boundary invariant and its geometric fibre

Lemma C.15.1.1. The actual restriction comparison \[ (j_*L)|_{\overline X_{\bar y}} \longrightarrow j_{\bar y,*}(L|_{U_{\bar y}}) \tag{C.15.1} \] is an isomorphism for every geometric \(\bar y\to Y_0\). At a geometric point \(d\) of the boundary its completed fibre is \(V^{I_d}\), where \(V\) is the local fibre of \(L\) and \(I_d\) is tame inertia. This statement uses the full \(E\)-system.

Proof. Locally along \(D_0\) there is a relative parameter \(z\) with \(D_0=(z)\). Indeed, trivialize its Cartier ideal near a point. Since \(D_0\to Y_0\) is étale, the image of \(dz\) generates the relative cotangent line along \(D_0\). After restricting to a smaller open, \((p_0,z)\) is étale over \(\mathbb A^1_{Y_0}\): the differential is invertible and both sides are smooth of the same relative dimension. Thus \(z\) has valuation one also on each geometric curve fibre.

The total space and divisor are regular. For the strict henselization \(A\) at \(d\), Theorem 2.3 just cited says that every finite tame cover of \(\operatorname{Spec}A[1/z]\) is a disjoint union of \[ \operatorname{Spec}A[1/z,t]/(t^m-z),\qquad (m,p)=1. \tag{C.15.2} \] Each displayed cover is connected: the valuation at the prime \((z)\) gives the degree \(m\) root-uniformizer calculation in that theorem. Its automorphisms are \(t\mapsto\zeta t\), for \(\zeta\in\mu_m(A)\); strict henselianity supplies these roots of unity. A common multiple of two exponents dominates both covers. Maps and automorphisms are determined by these root equations, so this describes the finite tame cover category, including its morphisms. Its pointed automorphism group is \(I_d=\varprojlim_{(m,p)=1}\mu_m=\widehat{\mathbb Z}^{(p')}(1)\).

Pull the equations to the strict local curve at the same geometric point. They remain connected Kummer covers of exactly degree \(m\), because the pulled parameter still has valuation one. The same description supplies every finite tame cover there and the same root maps between covers. Consequently restriction induces the identical tame inertia group and the identical action on every pulled finite coefficient fibre. This assertion applies to the entire compatible tower; no single finite cover is asserted to trivialize that tower.

There is a global stable lattice on each connected component of \(U_0\). Indeed \(U_0\) is smooth and hence geometrically unibranch. The cited Corollary 7.11 identifies its coefficient representation with a continuous representation of the profinite étale fundamental group. Its image is compact. The translates of a basis lattice lie in one larger lattice by compactness, and their \(O\)-span is a finite torsion-free \(O\)-module containing the starting lattice. It is a stable lattice. Let \(M\) be its local fibre and \(M_n=M/\pi^nM\).

Finite ordinary extension at \(d\) has degree-zero fibre \(M_n^{I_d}\). To see this directly, a finite section on the punctured strict local scheme is exactly a fibre vector fixed by the finite covering action. The Kummer comparison therefore identifies this fibre and its restriction to the curve fibre, with the actual restriction map.

Now apply O.2.1.1 to the lattice sheaf and M.6.2.1 to its geometric-point pullback. The complete fibre of its ordinary derived image is the coherent derived limit of the finite derived-image fibres. They are in degrees \(\ge0\); hence the degree-zero Milnor sequence has zero \(\varprojlim^1\mathcal H^{-1}\) term and gives \[ \mathcal H^0(d_O^*Rj_*L_O) =\varprojlim_n M_n^{I_d} =M^{I_d}. \tag{C.15.3} \] The last equality follows because compatible fixed vectors are precisely fixed vectors in \(M=\varprojlim_nM_n\). It is an inverse-limit assertion, not an assertion about reduction of \(M^{I_d}\).

Here \(d_O^*\) denotes actual completed ringed pullback; degree-zero cohomology is taken after that pullback. Actual rational ordinary image and actual rational pullback are the localizations of these integral constructions, by O.2.1.1 and M.6.2.2. Localization is exact. Choose a dense tame generator \(\tau\), and let \(T\) act on \(M\). Continuity makes invariance under \(\tau\) equivalent to invariance under \(I_d\), since the stabilizer equation is closed. Thus \[ M^{I_d}\otimes_O E =\ker(T-1:M\to M)\otimes_O E =\ker(T-1:V\to V)=V^{I_d}. \tag{C.15.4} \] Finite-level restriction was the same Kummer action, so its coherent limit and localization identify the actual map (C.15.1) with the identity on this invariant fibre. On the open curve it is already the identity. Ordinary rational inverse image is exact on constructible sheaves. On a lisse stratum the locally free \(E\)-coefficient model pulls back to the same degree-zero model. The finite closed-open filtration and the actual identities \(g^*i_*=i'_*g^*\), \(g^*j_!=j'_!g^*\) of M.6.1.2 extend this conclusion to the entire constructible sheaf, because those extensions and finite extensions of sheaves are exact. Closed restriction also has the coefficient identity M.3.4. Geometric fibres detect an isomorphism, using this finite stratal coefficient description. Therefore (C.15.1) is an isomorphism of ordinary sheaves. ∎

C.15.2. A finite logarithmic coefficient

On \(\mathbb G_m\) define a rank-\(n\) logarithmic system \(\operatorname{Log}_n\) with basis \(e_0,\ldots,e_{n-1}\) and \(Je_i=e_{i-1}\), \(Je_0=0\). Its tame inertia action is \[ a\longmapsto\exp(aJ),\qquad a\in\mathbb Z_\ell(1), \tag{C.15.5} \] after choosing a generator of the Tate line. The remaining prime-to-\(p\) inertia factors act trivially.

This is an actual complete coefficient system. The basis \(l_i=i!e_i\) is an \(O\)-lattice, and \[ \exp(aJ)l_i=\sum_{k=0}^{i}\binom{i}{k}a^k l_{i-k}. \tag{C.15.6} \] Its matrices are integral and continuous, so every finite reduction is a finite continuous action. The compatible \(\ell\)-power root covers of the coordinate construct this action geometrically. The integral classification in Theorem 7.10 and completion of its finite reductions construct the lattice sheaf; rationalization gives \(\operatorname{Log}_n\).

There is a Weil structure with geometric Frobenius \(Fe_i=q^ie_i\). For completeness, the root covers based at the section \(1\in\mathbb G_m\) give the Kummer quotient of its arithmetic fundamental group. Its geometric subgroup is \(\widehat{\mathbb Z}^{(p')}(1)\), and the section splits the projection to \(\widehat{\mathbb Z}\). Arithmetic Frobenius acts on roots of unity by \(q\), so geometric Frobenius conjugates inertia by \(q^{-1}\). The diagonal action just defined has \(FJF^{-1}=q^{-1}J\) and therefore respects that semidirect-product relation. It extends continuously to the arithmetic group: \(q\) is an \(\ell\)-adic unit, so its integer powers have finite images in every congruence quotient and extend to \(\widehat{\mathbb Z}\). This also proves continuity on the integral lattice (C.15.6). Thus \(J:\operatorname{Log}_n\to\operatorname{Log}_n(-1)\) is an actual Weil morphism. The inclusions \(e_i\mapsto e_i\) give compatible inclusions \(\operatorname{Log}_n\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Log}_{n+1}\).

On a parameter chart, pull this system back by \(z\) and put \[ B_n=\mathcal H^0i^*Rj_*(L\otimes z^*\operatorname{Log}_n), \qquad \mathcal N=-1\otimes J:B_n\to B_n(-1). \tag{C.15.7} \] These are constructible rational sheaves and actual morphisms by O.2.1.1 and M.6. In particular \(B_1=i^*j_*L=:B\).

Lemma C.15.2.1. If \(n\ge r\), the completed geometric fibre of \(B_n\) is the generalized eigenvalue-one part \(V_1\) of tame monodromy on \(V\). Under this identification \(\mathcal N\) is \(N=\log(T|_{V_1})\), with its Tate twist. The actual inclusion \(B\hookrightarrow B_n\) identifies \(B\) with \(\ker\mathcal N\). These identifications commute with geometric curve fibres, inclusions in \(n\), and Frobenius.

Proof. Normalize the chosen \(\ell\)-component of \(\tau\) to one. Lemma C.15.1.1 applied to the tensor coefficient gives the fibre \(\ker(T\otimes\exp J-1)\). The primary decomposition for the polynomial \(T\) separates \(V_1=\ker(T-1)^r\) from its complementary primary parts. On each other part, after a finite splitting field, the eigenvalues of \(T\otimes\exp J\) are the eigenvalues of \(T\), none equal to one. Its difference from one is invertible there, and faithful scalar extension proves that the invariant kernel has no such part over \(E\).

On \(V_1\) the finite logarithm \(N=\log T\) is nilpotent, with \(N^r=0\), and \[ T\otimes\exp J=\exp(N\otimes1+1\otimes J). \] For a nilpotent operator \(A\), \(\exp A-1=A\,h(A)\), where \(h(0)=1\); the polynomial \(h(A)\) is invertible by a finite geometric series. Hence the required kernel is \(\ker(N\otimes1+1\otimes J)\).

There is an explicit inverse pair \[ \Psi_n:V_1\xrightarrow{\sim}(B_n)_d^{\mathrm{comp}}, \qquad \Psi_n(v)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-N)^iv\otimes e_i, \tag{C.15.8} \] and extraction of the coefficient of \(e_0\). Indeed a tensor \(\sum v_i\otimes e_i\) is in the kernel exactly when \(Nv_i+v_{i+1}=0\) for \(i<n-1\) and \(Nv_{n-1}=0\). These equations determine all coefficients from \(v_0\), and the last equation is automatic for \(n\ge r\). The formulas also show that the inclusion for \(n\to n+1\) is an isomorphism on these kernels: its last new coefficient is \((-N)^nv=0\).

The coefficient of \(e_0\) in \(-1\otimes J\) applied to (C.15.8) is \(-v_1=Nv\). Thus \(\mathcal N\) corresponds to \(N\). Its kernel consists exactly of tensors \(v\otimes e_0\) with \(Nv=0\); this is the actual map from \(B_1\). Equality of the completed fibres proves the claimed sheaf kernel identity. The logarithmic system is tame, so C.15.1.1 applies to it and gives the geometric fibre compatibility of all these actual maps.

At a closed boundary point of residue cardinality \(q_d\), Frobenius preserves \(V_1\). Conjugation acts on tame inertia by its invertible \(q_d^{-1}\)-power automorphism; it therefore preserves the subrepresentation on which inertia is unipotent. Taking the finite logarithm there gives \(FNF^{-1}=q_d^{-1}N\). The logarithmic coefficient has \(Fe_i=q_d^ie_i\). Consequently \[ F\Psi_n(v)=\sum_i(-q_d^{-1}N)^iFv\otimes q_d^ie_i =\Psi_n(Fv). \tag{C.15.9} \] This verifies the Frobenius action and the sign in (C.15.7). No semisimplicity of Frobenius and no finite cover killing all the monodromy of \(L\) were assumed. ∎

C.15.3. Actual monodromy subsheaves after one base restriction

Theorem C.15.3.1. After replacing \(Y_0\) by a dense open, \(B=i^*j_*L\) is lisse and has a finite increasing filtration by actual lisse subsheaves whose completed geometric fibres are the monodromy filtration induced on \(V^{I_d}\), centred at zero on the unipotent part \(V_1\). Its formation commutes with geometric curve fibres. For a Weil \(L\), the filtration is Frobenius stable. Thus both relative comparisons required in Weights, purity and semisimplicity over finite fields, Appendix D, Lemma D2, hold for this tame curve family on that dense open.

Proof. Cover the boundary by finitely many parameter opens of \(\overline X_0\), as in C.15.1. On each boundary open the single sheaf \(B_r\) of C.15.7 is constructible. Choose a dense open in each of its irreducible components on which it is lisse; this is the finite stratification in the definition of rational constructibility. The closure in \(D_0\) of each discarded subset contains no generic point of a component of \(D_0\). There are only finitely many charts. Since \(D_0\to Y_0\) is finite, the images of these closures are closed; they omit the generic point of \(Y_0\), because a proper closed subset of a finite dominant component cannot dominate an integral base. Delete their finite union from \(Y_0\). Every \(B_r\) on the restricted charts is now lisse.

An actual morphism between two lisse finite-dimensional \(E\)-systems is locally its fixed coefficient matrix. Its kernel and image are lisse systems: they are the corresponding invariant subspaces under the local representation, and the representation classification constructs their inclusions and quotient. Exactness on fibres follows from exact linear algebra over \(E\). Apply this to \(\mathcal N\) and its powers, retaining Tate twists. Lemma C.15.2.1 identifies \(B=\ker\mathcal N\), so \(B\) is lisse as well.

For \(k\le0\) define on such a chart \[ B_{\le k} =\ker\mathcal N\ \cap\ \operatorname{im}\bigl(\mathcal N^{-k}(-k): B_r(-k)\longrightarrow B_r\bigr); \qquad B_{\le k}=B\quad(k\ge0). \tag{C.15.10} \] The superscript is the nonnegative integer power \(-k\); the parenthesized twist brings its target back to \(B_r\). These are actual lisse subsheaves of the same \(B\). They are zero for \(k<-(r-1)\), so only finitely many terms differ. Kernels, images and intersections are exact on the lisse fibres, and C.15.2.1 identifies their fibres with \[ \ker N\cap\operatorname{im}N^{-k}\quad(k\le0). \tag{C.15.11} \]

Here is the linear algebra identifying this filtration. For a nilpotent \(N\), set \[ M_kV_1= \sum_{a=0}^{r-1} \bigl(\ker N^{a+1}\cap\operatorname{im}N^{a-k}\bigr), \tag{C.15.12} \] where an image with exponent \(\le0\) means the whole space. Kernels and images make this formula independent of a choice of Jordan basis. On a chain \(v_0,\ldots,v_{h-1}\) with \(Nv_i=v_{i-1}\), its term indexed by \(a\) is spanned by \[ v_0,\ldots,v_{\min(a,h-1-\max(a-k,0))}, \] with the term zero when this upper index is negative. Maximizing this upper index shows that \(v_i\) enters exactly when \(2i-(h-1)\le k\). Thus the successive chain grades are \(-(h-1),-(h-3),\ldots,h-1\).
