Closedness would put that origin in the nonregular inverse image. This contradicts the principal origin value just proved. The inverse image is empty, so every geometric value is regular. ∎

The common lower lattice retains the full finite quotients. The moving line transports its degree-two Tate-valued cup map through the collision. Its nonzero top IC cup power forces every simple-root coefficient at the origin, and positive grading propagates regularity. Proofs C.19.1.1–C.19.6.1. The disc symbols and the final grading arrow are schematics of those proved comparisons. Editable SVG source.

C.19.7. Two determinant-operator exercises

Exercise C.19.7.1 (basic). Take \(G=\mathbb G_m\) and its identity character representation. Compute the determinant character and primitive cup action on the point-supported object at label \(n\), and compare labels \(n,m,n+m\).

Solution. For \(N>|n|\), the two ranks in (C.19.1) are \(N-n,N\), so their character difference is \(n\chi\). If \(u=c_1(\chi)\), the action on its module \(S\) is multiplication by \(nu\), with its Tate line. Labels add under convolution, and \((n+m)u=nu+mu\) is exactly (C.19.7). At label zero the line has its canonical unit frame and the operator is zero. These supported cohomology objects use the reduced points, while the line was constructed on the full torus Grassmannian including its nilpotent directions. Integrally the operator \(nu\) alone does not recover the degree-zero label operator \(n\) by division by \(u\); that division requires localization.

Exercise C.19.7.2 (intermediate). Take \(G=GL_2\), its standard representation, and the minuscule \(P=E_{\mathbb P^1}[1]\). Choose a Tate vector for the scalar formulas. Let \(u_1,u_2\) be the two character classes and \(\sigma_1=u_1+u_2,\sigma_2=u_1u_2\). Compute the determinant cup matrix and its normalized rank-one companion form.

Solution. At the support \(tO^2\subset L\subset O^2\), the tautological line is \(L/tO^2\). Formula (C.19.1) is its inverse times \(\det(k^2)\). If its class is \(a\), put \(x=\sigma_1-a\). The relative flag Chern-basis calculation of C.14 gives \[ H_T^*(\mathbb P^1,E)= S[a]/((a-u_1)(a-u_2)) =S[x]/(x^2-\sigma_1x+\sigma_2). \] The two fixed values of \(x\) are \(u_1,u_2\), agreeing with (C.19.9). The invariant basis \(1,x\) descends through C.14 to the \(R_0=E[\sigma_1,\sigma_2]\)-basis of \(H_G^*(P)\), in degrees \(-1,1\). Actual cup with \(x\) has matrix \[ \begin{pmatrix}0&-\sigma_2\\1&\sigma_1\end{pmatrix}. \tag{C.19.18} \] Set \(y=x-\sigma_1/2\) and \(c=\sigma_1^2/4-\sigma_2\). In the ordered homogeneous basis \((y,1)\), of degrees \(1,-1\), the matrix becomes \[ \frac{\sigma_1}{2}I+ \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\c&0\end{pmatrix}. \tag{C.19.19} \] The second matrix is the rank-one Kostant companion, with \(c\) of degree four; the scalar part retains the full \(GL_2\) centre. At the coefficient origin its raising map is nonzero and its square is zero. The eigenvalues are \(u_1,u_2\), and their difference is nonzero after localization. The basis change is graded and has determinant a unit. These are actual cup matrices on this geometric module; they do not identify all higher operators for a general group.

C.20. Fixed-point weights and the actual Kostant quotient

Retain the coefficient rings, full dual group \(H\), faithful representation \(\rho\), and actual determinant cup element of Sections 17–19. In this section choose one nonzero vector of \(E(1)\) for the scalar Lie and polynomial formulas. The conclusions with their Tate lines follow by undoing that choice. Write \[ L=X_*(T_G)=X^*(T_H),\qquad \mathfrak t_G=X_*(T_G)\otimes E,\qquad \mathfrak t_H=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(L,E). \tag{C.20.1} \] The trace pairing from C.19.4 defines the isomorphism \[ \beta_\rho:\mathfrak t_G\longrightarrow\mathfrak t_H,\qquad \lambda(\beta_\rho(u)) =\sum_{\chi\text{ a weight of }V_\rho} \langle\chi,\lambda\rangle\,\chi(u). \tag{C.20.2} \] Multiplicities occur in this sum. It is \(W\)-equivariant and invertible, including on the centre, by C.19.4. The cohomological degree of a linear coordinate is two. Thus \(\beta_\rho\) identifies the positively graded quotient bases. We first check which multiplicities occur in the localized geometric operator.

The first panel concerns the reduced cohomological support of the finite attracting scheme: invertible character coordinates kill its punctured compact image only after extension to \(Q\). The second uses the resulting actual weight multiplicities to test every invariant polynomial and then descends along the injective coefficient map. The third shows the full smooth conjugation scheme and the precise grading sign that permits a polynomial section through the identity. Proofs C.20.1.1–C.20.4.1 establish these maps; Exercise C.20.5.2 explains why adapting the rational pinning matters. Editable SVG source.

C.20.1. Compact attraction computes the fixed-point rank

Lemma C.20.1.1. For a raw spherical \(P=IC_\lambda\), supported on a finite projective Schubert variety \(X\), and any torus label \(\mu\), there is an actual restriction isomorphism \[ Q\otimes_S H_{T,c}^*(S_\mu\cap X,P) \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ } Q\otimes_S H_T^*(t^\mu,i_\mu^*P), \qquad Q=\operatorname{Frac}S. \tag{C.20.3} \] An empty slice gives zero on both sides. In particular \[ \dim_E F_\mu(IC_\lambda) =\sum_j\dim_E H^j(i_\mu^*IC_\lambda). \tag{C.20.4} \] Under the full Satake equivalence, this is the multiplicity of weight \(\mu\) in \(V_\lambda\).

Proof. The finite projective lattice embedding is \(T\)-equivariant. Choose a strictly dominant constant cocharacter \(\gamma\) which does not annihilate any nonzero difference of the finitely many ambient weights. Such an integral choice exists: the strictly dominant rational cone is open, and finitely many proper rational hyperplanes cannot cover it. The root-parameter calculation of Lesson 5, §7, equation (7.1), applies to this \(\gamma\). Its positive root powers contract every point of \(S_\mu\) to \(t^\mu\); the proved Iwasawa partition and uniqueness of a limit show that this is precisely the corresponding attracting locus.

Here is a finite coordinate realization sufficient for localization. Choose an eigen-coordinate \(z_0\) nonzero at the fixed point in the projective embedding. Its chart is \(T\)-stable. If \(\eta_i\) is the weight of the ratio \(z_i/z_0\), attraction to this point sets the negative \(\gamma\)-weight ratios to zero and the zero-weight ratios to their point values. The remaining ratios \(x_i\) have \(\langle\eta_i,\gamma\rangle>0\). Intersecting these equations with the closed equations of \(X\) gives a closed affine attracting scheme \(A\), with distinguished origin. The description holds on every parameter algebra: an equivariant polynomial path has coordinate \(s^{\langle\eta_i,\gamma\rangle}x_i\); a negative power belongs to \(R[s]\) only when its coefficient is zero, including for nilpotent coefficients. The underlying reduced locus is \(S_\mu\cap X\), by the preceding partition. Actual closed-reduction comparison in C.12 and Lesson 5 O.4.2.1 identifies their cohomology and compact images, so we may use this finite scheme.

The finitely many invariant opens \(D(x_i)\) cover \(A\setminus\{t^\mu\}\). Each \(\eta_i\) is a nonzero character. On \(D(x_i)\) the function \(x_i\) is an invertible equivariant section of the line with character \(\eta_i\), up to the harmless inverse convention for the function action. Its first Chern class is therefore zero on that open. This is an actual equivariant line trivialization: after tensoring by the inverse character, the section has weight zero and gives the trivial frame on the whole action nerve. The Kummer cocycle of that frame is zero. Consequently the nonzero linear class \(c_1(\eta_i)\), or its negative, acts by zero on the compact equivariant cohomology of any restricted coefficient complex there.

The same argument works on every intersection in this finite open cover. Localization to \(Q\) therefore kills all these compact cohomology modules. The compact Mayer–Vietoris triangle follows from the two extension-by-zero maps and their overlap, checked on stalks; induction on the number of opens gives \[ Q\otimes_S H_{T,c}^*(A\setminus\{t^\mu\},P)=0. \tag{C.20.5} \] The compact image of the actual open–closed triangle \(j_!j^*P\to P\to i_{\mu*}i_\mu^*P\) now gives exactly (C.20.3). Localization is flat, so this conclusion preserves the specified restriction map.

To compute its two ranks, form the genuine \(T\)-equivariant compact image of \(P|_A\) on the point. Its underlying complex is concentrated in degree \(h_\mu=\langle2\rho_G,\mu\rangle\), with coefficient \(F_\mu(P)\), by Lesson 5, Theorem Q.4.2.1. A complex with one ordinary cohomology sheaf is canonically that sheaf shifted. The equivariant point sheaf here is constant: its action matrix is a degree-zero section on the connected torus, hence is a constant matrix, and the identity section forces that matrix to be the identity. Its cocycle is consequently the constant cocycle. Taking equivariant cohomology gives \[ H_{T,c}^*(A,P)=S\otimes_E F_\mu(P)[-h_\mu]. \tag{C.20.6} \] This computation uses the actual compact image object, whose construction on the action nerve commutes with the smooth atlas comparisons; it does not assert nonproper ordinary base change.

At the fixed point, the ordinary stalk of \(IC_\lambda\) has one parity by Lesson 11 C.4.1.1. Its finite equivariant ordinary tower splits as in C.10: an attaching map between constant even-separated terms belongs to an odd group of \(S\), which is zero. Thus its equivariant cohomology is a free \(S\)-module of rank \(\sum_j\dim H^j(i_\mu^*P)\). Comparing ranks in (C.20.3) proves (C.20.4). Finally the full weight identification in Lesson 11 B.1–B.4 identifies \(F_\mu(IC_\lambda)\) with the \(\mu\)-weight space of \(V_\lambda\). This proves the last assertion. ∎

C.20.2. Traces test every invariant polynomial

Lemma C.20.2.1. Let \(H\) be the original split connected reductive group in characteristic zero, with its full character lattice \(L\). In each positive polynomial degree \(n\), the functions \[ x\longmapsto\operatorname{tr}_{V_\lambda}(x^n), \qquad \lambda\in L\text{ dominant}, \tag{C.20.7} \] span \(E[\mathfrak h]^H_n\). No change to a simply connected cover is required.

Proof. First work on \(\mathfrak t_H\). Integral linear forms \(\mu\in L\) span its linear dual. Their \(n\)-th powers span all degree-\(n\) polynomials, because \[ n!\,\ell_1\cdots\ell_n =\sum_{J\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}} (-1)^{n-|J|} \left(\sum_{j\in J}\ell_j\right)^n. \tag{C.20.8} \] Expansion proves this identity: any monomial omitting an index cancels in the subset sum, and the sole monomial using every index has coefficient \(n!\). Choose the \(\ell_j\) from an integral basis and use linearity. Averaging over the finite Weyl group therefore shows that the invariant polynomials are spanned by \(\sum_{\mu\in W\lambda}\mu^n\) for dominant \(\lambda\in L\), with distinct orbit elements.

The formal character of \(V_\lambda\) is the orbit sum of \(\lambda\), with coefficient one, plus a finite combination of orbit sums of strictly lower dominant weights. This is the highest-weight theorem of Lesson 11: the extremal weights \(W\lambda\) have multiplicity one, and all other weights differ from \(\lambda\) by positive-root sums. This triangular relation can be inverted using only representations of the original group. To see termination, pair the dominant weights with the sum of positive coroots of the derived group. The resulting nonnegative integers strictly decrease under a strict dominance drop. All terms keep the same central character. For a torus there are no lower terms. Thus induction expresses every orbit sum as a finite linear combination of these full-group characters.

Apply the linear operation \(e^\mu\mapsto\mu^n\) to that character identity. The character of \(V_\lambda\) becomes the torus restriction of (C.20.7), because a torus Lie element acts by the scalar \(\mu(x)\) on its \(\mu\)-weight space. Hence those trace functions span the torus invariants. Chevalley restriction and its full reductive centre, including the descent to every characteristic-zero coefficient field proved in §3 of this lesson, identify the latter with \(E[\mathfrak h]^H\). The trace polynomials are themselves conjugation invariant, so the spanning assertion follows. ∎

C.20.3. The quotient is the trace-pairing coordinate map

Theorem C.20.3.1. The actual geometric element satisfies, for every invariant polynomial \(f\), \[ f(\xi_\rho)=f(\beta_\rho(u)) \quad\text{in }R_0=E[\mathfrak t_G]^W. \tag{C.20.9} \] Here the right side denotes restriction of \(f\) to \(\mathfrak t_H\), followed by \(\beta_\rho\). Thus its adjoint quotient is the specified isomorphism of the coefficient base with \(\mathfrak h/\!/H\).

Proof. Extend the actual cup action and its graded tensor trivialization to \(S\), and then to \(Q\). C.19.4 gives the actual fixed-point decomposition and projectors. On the block at \(\mu\) the determinant cup element acts by \[ \nu_\mu(u)=\mu(\beta_\rho(u)). \tag{C.20.10} \] These eigenvalues are distinct for distinct labels, since \(\beta_\rho\) is an isomorphism. Lemma C.20.1.1 identifies the dimension of that block with \(\dim V_\lambda[\mu]\). Consequently, for every \(n>0\), \[ \operatorname{tr}_{V_\lambda}(\xi_\rho^n) =\sum_\mu\dim V_\lambda[\mu]\, \mu(\beta_\rho(u))^n =\operatorname{tr}_{V_\lambda}(\beta_\rho(u)^n) \quad\text{in }Q. \tag{C.20.11} \] The first trace is taken on the finite free module \(\omega_G(IC_\lambda)\); under C.18 it is precisely the represented Lie action on \(R_0\otimes V_\lambda\). The actual torus restriction and fixed-point projectors computed its trace, rather than substituting an abstract module with the same rank.

Lemma C.20.2.1 gives (C.20.9) for homogeneous invariant polynomials of positive degree. Constants agree as well, and finite sums handle every polynomial. Both sides lie in \(R_0\). Its inclusion into \(S\), and into \(Q\), is injective by C.14, so the equality already holds in \(R_0\). Since \(\beta_\rho\) is invertible and \(W\)-equivariant, its map on the two invariant polynomial rings is an isomorphism. This proves the precise quotient statement over every coefficient parameter algebra, including nonreduced ones. ∎

C.20.4. A rational pinning and a graded conjugation

Theorem C.20.4.1. Adapt the pinning of \(H\) to the nonzero simple coefficients of \(\xi_\rho(0)\). Let \(\sigma_\rho:\mathfrak h/\!/H\to\mathfrak h_{\mathrm{reg}}\) be the resulting Kostant section. There is a graded tensor trivialization, with the same actual augmentation as C.18, in which the determinant cup element is exactly \[ \sigma_\rho\!\left(\beta_\rho(u)\bmod W\right). \tag{C.20.12} \] The construction preserves the original full dual group and centre. It need not give a conjugation to an arbitrarily prescribed old pinning over \(E\).

Proof. By C.19.6 write \(e_\rho=\xi_\rho(0)=\sum_i a_i e_i\), with every \(a_i\in E^\times\). Set \[ e_i^\rho=a_i e_i,\qquad f_i^\rho=a_i^{-1}f_i,\qquad f_\rho=\sum_i c_i f_i^\rho . \tag{C.20.13} \] Their principal triple has the same \(h=2\rho_H^\vee\) and cocharacter \(\vartheta\) as B.2. These are a full rational pinning, not a claim about an \(H(E)\)-conjugator. Indeed the simple characters form a basis on the adjoint torus, so the tuple \((a_i)\) gives an \(E\)-point there. After a finite extension it lifts to the original split torus: in character bases, Smith normal form reduces the lift to finitely many equations \(z^m=a\), all with \(a\ne0\). The corresponding extensions are separable in characteristic zero. Two lifts differ by the centre and hence give the same conjugation on \(H\). The resulting conjugation automorphism and its inverse descend to \(E\); the matrix coefficients agree on the two overlap projections, so affine faithful-flat descent supplies the descended maps. Denote this automorphism by \(\phi_\rho\). It fixes the torus, the centre, \(\vartheta\), and every invariant polynomial. The last assertion can be tested after that finite extension, where it is ordinary inner conjugation, and then descends injectively.

Apply \(\phi_\rho\) to the complete proofs of B.2–B.3. The adapted slice is \(\Sigma_\rho=e_\rho+\ker\operatorname{ad}f_\rho\). Its quotient is the same polynomial isomorphism on all parameter algebras; its positive action is still \(t^2\operatorname{Ad}_{\vartheta(t^{-1})}\). Its inverse defines \(\sigma_\rho\). The full map \(H\times\Sigma_\rho\to\mathfrak h_{\mathrm{reg}}\) is smooth surjective. In the torus case \(\phi_\rho\) is the identity and the slice is all of \(\mathfrak h\); the argument includes it.

Let \(B=\operatorname{Spec}R_0\), and put \(s(p)=\sigma_\rho(\beta_\rho(p)\bmod W)\). Consider the affine scheme of finite presentation \[ P=\{(p,g)\in B\times H: \operatorname{Ad}_g s(p)=\xi_\rho(p)\}. \tag{C.20.14} \] It is the base change of the preceding smooth surjection along \(\xi_\rho:B\to\mathfrak h_{\mathrm{reg}}\). To check this scheme equality, the base change initially has a variable slice point \(\sigma\). Applying the quotient to its defining equality forces \(\sigma=s(p)\), by (C.20.9) and the polynomial inverse on the full slice. This argument holds for every parameter algebra. Thus \(P\to B\) is smooth surjective. Its fibres retain the full centralizer, including its centre and all components.

Homogeneity of \(\xi_\rho\) and of the slice gives the algebraic action \[ t\cdot(p,g) =\bigl(t\cdot p,\, \vartheta(t^{-1})g\vartheta(t)\bigr) \quad\text{on }P. \tag{C.20.15} \] Substitution in (C.20.14) proves this identity, including its sign: both \(s(t\cdot p)\) and \(\xi_\rho(t\cdot p)\) equal \(t^2\operatorname{Ad}_{\vartheta(t^{-1})}\) of their values at \(p\). The point \((0,1)\) is fixed, since \(s(0)=e_\rho=\xi_\rho(0)\). The base coordinates have positive weights. Lemma C.18.1.1 therefore gives an actual polynomial equivariant section \(g:B\to H\) through the identity, by its finite-order lifts and homogeneous stabilization. No field-point lifting or merely formal section is substituted.

If \(\tau\) is the former tensor trivialization, replace it on each representation by \(g^{-1}\tau\). The represented group action is a natural tensor automorphism on every test algebra. Equation (C.20.15) makes the new frame graded; its identity value at the origin retains the actual augmentation. Finally \(\operatorname{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\xi_\rho=s\) is exactly (C.20.12). The higher primitive operators still have to be constructed and identified with the centralizer gradients. ∎

C.20.5. Two quotient and pinning exercises

Exercise C.20.5.1 (basic). For \(GL_2\) with its standard trace pairing, recover the two adjoint-quotient coordinates from the actual matrix C.19.18. Check that the centre survives the comparison.

Solution. Here \(\beta_\rho\) is the identity on the two diagonal coordinates. The matrix has trace \(\sigma_1\), determinant \(\sigma_2\), and squared trace invariant \(\operatorname{tr}(\xi_\rho^2)=\sigma_1^2-2\sigma_2\). Hence its characteristic polynomial is \(z^2-\sigma_1z+\sigma_2\), exactly the polynomial of the diagonal pair \((u_1,u_2)\). The central scalar in C.19.19 is \(\sigma_1/2\); it is retained by this quotient map. Trace and squared trace generate the same invariant algebra as trace and determinant since \(2\) is invertible in \(E\).

Exercise C.20.5.2 (intermediate). In \(SL_2\) over a characteristic-zero coefficient field \(E\), let \(a\in E^\times\). Determine when \(aE_{12}\) is conjugate to \(E_{12}\) by an \(SL_2(E)\)-matrix. Construct its adapted principal triple even when that conjugator does not exist.

Solution. Any such conjugator preserves the unique kernel line \(Ee_1\), so has the form \(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}z&b\\0&z^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). Its conjugation sends \(E_{12}\) to \(z^2E_{12}\). A conjugator exists exactly when \(a\) is a square in \(E^\times\). For any \(a\ne0\), however, the vectors \(e_a=aE_{12}\), \(f_a=a^{-1}E_{21}\), and \(h=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)\) satisfy \([e_a,f_a]=h\), \([h,e_a]=2e_a\), and \([h,f_a]=-2f_a\). They define the adapted slice \(e_a+Ef_a\) with the same invariant quotient. Its adjoint-torus rescaling descends over \(E\) even if its original-torus lift requires adjoining a square root. This is the precise distinction used in C.20.4.

C.21. Higher primitive cup operators and the generator modules

Keep the adapted graded tensor frame of C.20.4. Choose a Tate vector as in C.20 for scalar formulas. For a homogeneous coefficient class \(q\in R_0\) of polynomial degree \(d\geq1\), its cohomological degree and natural twist are \(2d\) and \(E(d)\). We construct a cup class of degree \(2d-2\), with twist \(E(d-1)\). Its normalization will be a directional derivative, including in polynomial degree one.

The first panel is the exact torus-fixed calculation on the curve, with its negative lattice degree and trace normalization. The second uses the full framed moving torsor to obtain the primitive operator before identifying its gradient. The third displays the two coefficient descents that recover linearity over every whole-Grassmannian class. Here the diagram abbreviation \(B_{\mathrm{op}}\) denotes the polynomial operator algebra \(\mathcal B_\rho\), while the inverse trace form is \(B_H\). Proofs C.21.1.1–C.21.5.1 supply the maps; the coherent Hom uses the deformation grading of B.5. Editable SVG source.

C.21.1. The inverse trace form on the full dual Lie algebra

Lemma C.21.1.1. There is a nondegenerate \(H\)-invariant symmetric bilinear form \(B_H\) on \(\mathfrak h\) whose restriction to \(\mathfrak t_H\) is \[ B_H(v,w)=B_\rho(\beta_\rho^{-1}v,\beta_\rho^{-1}w). \tag{C.21.1} \] For \(f=q\circ\beta_\rho^{-1}\), regarded as an invariant polynomial on \(\mathfrak h\) by Chevalley restriction, its gradient satisfies \[ \lambda\!\left(\nabla_{B_H}f(\beta_\rho(u))\right) =D_\lambda q(u)\qquad(\lambda\in L). \tag{C.21.2} \]

Proof. The displayed Cartan form is nondegenerate and \(W\)-invariant by C.19.4. It is orthogonal between distinct derived simple components, and between the derived Cartan and the centre. Indeed a reflection fixes the other component and the centre, while negating its simple coroot; invariance makes the pairing with that coroot zero. The simple coroots span the derived Cartan.

On one simple component, compare this form with the Cartan restriction of the Killing form. Its Cartan matrix is the root Gram form \(\sum_{\alpha}\alpha\otimes\alpha\): these roots span the rational Cartan dual, and the real Gram form is positive definite. Its determinant is therefore a nonzero rational number, hence remains nonzero over every characteristic-zero \(E\). The Killing form is invariant by the trace-of-a-commutator identity. Distinct root spaces pair only when their roots are opposite, by Cartan invariance. For a normalized root pair its value is \(K(e_\alpha,f_\alpha)=K(\alpha^\vee,\alpha^\vee)/2\), again nonzero by that rational Gram calculation. This also verifies nondegeneracy on the full simple Lie ideal. The comparison endomorphism commutes with every reflection. A reflection's negative eigenspace is its simple-coroot line, so the endomorphism has some scalar on each such line. For adjacent simple coroots, the formula \(s_i(\alpha_j^\vee)=\alpha_j^\vee-m_{ij}\alpha_i^\vee\), with \(m_{ij}\ne0\), forces their scalars to agree. The connected simple Dynkin diagram makes the comparison a single scalar. That scalar is nonzero by nondegeneracy. Extend by this scalar times the Killing form on each simple Lie ideal, and by the prescribed form on the centre. This constructs the required full nondegenerate invariant form; neither a central direction nor a simple factor is discarded. Invariance for the algebraic group follows from the same torus and root-exponential argument as §3: a Lie-invariant polynomial tensor is torus-invariant and root-group-invariant, and those subgroups generate \(H\).

Put \(v=\beta_\rho(u)\), and let \(b=\beta_\rho^{-1}\nabla_{B_H}f(v)\) in the Cartan. The gradient lies there: invariant gradients are fixed by the torus stabilizing \(v\), so this holds on the dense regular Cartan and then as a polynomial identity on the entire Cartan. For \(x\in\mathfrak t_G\), the gradient definition gives \[ B_\rho(b,x) =B_H(\nabla f(v),\beta_\rho(x)) =df_v(\beta_\rho(x)) =dq_u(x). \tag{C.21.3} \] By C.20.2, \(\lambda(\beta_\rho(b))=B_\rho(\lambda,b)\). Substituting \(x=\lambda\) in (C.21.3) proves (C.21.2). The equality extends from the regular Cartan to all its points because both sides are polynomials. ∎

C.21.2. A framed characteristic class and its primitive cup map

Theorem C.21.2.1. There is a compatible actual whole-Grassmannian equivariant class \[ z_q\in H_G^{2d-2}(\operatorname{Gr}_G,E(d-1)). \tag{C.21.4} \] Its cup maps on the spherical cohomology modules are natural and \(R_0\)-linear, vanish on the tensor unit, and satisfy \[ z_q|_{\omega_G(P*Q)} =z_q|_{\omega_G(P)}\otimes1 +1\otimes z_q|_{\omega_G(Q)} \tag{C.21.5} \] under the actual tensor comparison C.17. The class is linear in \(q\). The construction uses the full lattice and moving-chain functors before taking their cohomological supports.

Proof. Use \(\mathbb P^1\) with modification point \(0\). The full gluing equivalence of Lesson 3, Proposition 2.1 and Theorem 3.1, gives on \(\mathbb P^1\times\operatorname{Gr}_G\) the universal right \(G\)-torsor \(\mathcal P\), with its specified frame off \(0\). This assertion can be read stage by stage: pull back to the local loop-frame covers, glue the disc torsor to the framed trivial torsor on the complement, and descend along the exact change-of-frame maps. The gluing equivalence includes morphisms, so these transitions satisfy the cocycle on the full parameter algebras. A framed automorphism which is the identity off \(0\) is the identity: on a local trivialization its defining functions vanish after inverting the curve parameter, which is a nonzerodivisor on the flat torsor. Thus there is no residual automorphism ambiguity in this descent.

The constant \(G\)-action is retained. Let \(\mathcal P_{\mathrm{ref}}\) be the trivial curve torsor with its diagonal constant-\(G\) equivariance. Its characteristic class is the base class \(q\); its ordinary bundle being trivial does not make its equivariant class zero. Characteristic classes here mean actual pullback from the \(G\)-torsor classifying nerve: the element \(q\in H_G^{2d}(\mathrm{pt},E(d))\) pulls back along each torsor's classifying map. The off-point frame gives a specified isomorphism of these torsors there.

We spell out how that frame supplies a supported class, rather than choosing a lift of a vanishing restriction. In the completed derived mapping models of Lesson 5, M.6 and Lemma P.1.3.1, use the relative-cone construction of Lesson 6 A.2 and represent \(q\) by a closed cochain. Write \(F_b,F_r\) for its two classifying pullback chain maps. On the complement \(U\) the framed torsor isomorphism gives a chain homotopy \(K\) with \[ (F_b-F_r)|_U=dK+Kd. \tag{C.21.6} \] It is the ordinary nerve prism: in a string of composable transition arrows insert the isomorphism in each possible position and take the alternating sum. All inner faces cancel in pairs; the two remaining end faces are the two classifying maps. This verifies (C.21.6) directly. On a common trivializing cover the formula is finite in every nerve degree. Refining that cover preserves the formula, and the same prism for change of cover identifies its augmentations. Étale descent and the completed inverse limits retain these finite identities. Hence it defines the specified homotopy in the genuine equivariant enhancement, with all action faces and degeneracies.

In the relative complex \(\operatorname{Cone}(C(\mathbb P^1\times X)\to C(U))[-1]\) of any finite stage, use the pair \[ c_q=\big((F_b-F_r)q,Kq\big),\qquad d(c,u)=(dc,c|_U-du). \tag{C.21.7} \] It is closed by (C.21.6). Replacing \(q\) by \(q+dv\) changes it by the relative boundary of \(((F_b-F_r)v,Kv)\), since \(Kdv=(F_b-F_r)v|_U-dKv\). Thus its supported class is well defined and linear in the original cohomology class. The construction commutes with parameter pullback, change of frame and the constant-\(G\) nerve maps through their actual prism homotopies.

Let \(\pi:\mathbb P^1\times X\to X\). Forget the support in (C.21.7), and apply the actual smooth proper curve trace of Lesson 5, Theorems P.2.3.1–P.2.4.1, with the Kummer orientation normalized in Lesson 6 A.1.1. Define \[ z_q=-\pi_*\bigl(q(\mathcal P)-q(\mathcal P_{\mathrm{ref}})\bigr). \tag{C.21.8} \] The minus sign belongs to the lattice convention and is checked below. This trace lowers degree by two and twist by one, proving the degree in (C.21.4). The supported construction remains available to compare sums of modifications. Proper base change and the trace comparisons make these classes compatible on every finite stage and on all action-nerve levels. C.12 identifies these compatible classes with an actual class of the whole ind-Grassmannian, including all its component products.

For primitivity, construct the same framed relative class on \(\mathbb P^1\times Y_r\) for the full moving ordered chain of Lesson 8. Its support is the union of the graphs of the moving points. Off the diagonals those graphs are disjoint. Cochains supported on a finite disjoint union split as the direct sum of the individual support complexes: the corresponding closed support functor is the sum of their extension-by-zero functors, an equality checked on stalks. On the open obtained by deleting the other graphs, the full gluing and CRT comparison of Lesson 8 C.1–C.6 identifies the last framed torsor with the single input modification. Consequently the prism class restricts to that input's prism class on each support summand. Curve trace then gives the sum of the input \(z_q\)'s.

On a full collision the last framed torsor is the endpoint pullback of \(\mathcal P\); its off-point frame is the endpoint frame. Proper curve base change therefore identifies the same moving class with the endpoint pullback of (C.21.8). These statements concern the actual maps on the full families and their nilpotent parameters.

Cup with the moving class gives the actual morphism of the moving coefficient complex, shifted by \(2d-2\) and twisted by \(d-1\). Take its proper image on the full action nerve as in C.17.4. The canonical affine counits of C.17.3 transport this morphism to the base: both its source and its shifted target belong to the fully faithful essential image. Thus its lift is unique. Restriction to distinct sections is the sum just proved, and restriction to collision is the endpoint cup map. All finite block maps, action faces and degeneracies commute with those counits. Derived sections and the actual external cup comparison give (C.21.5). This repeats the morphism-level transport of C.19.3 with its actual higher degree and twist. At the zero modification the framed torsors are identical; their prism difference is zero, proving the unit assertion. ∎

C.21.3. The fixed-point derivative and the lattice sign

Lemma C.21.3.1. Restriction to a torus point is \[ z_q|_{t^\lambda}=D_\lambda q(u)\in S, \qquad \lambda\in L. \tag{C.21.9} \] The natural twist of this equality is \(E(d-1)\).

Proof. At \(t^\lambda\) the universal bundle has its torus reduction. For a character \(\chi\), its associated line on the curve is \[ \mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1} (-\langle\chi,\lambda\rangle\,0)\otimes k_\chi, \tag{C.21.10} \] where \(k_\chi\) denotes the geometric character line. The negative sign is exactly the full lattice gluing convention of Lesson 3, §3. Its equivariant first Chern class is \(\chi(u)-\langle\chi,\lambda\rangle h\), where \(h=c_1(\mathcal O(1))\).

The curve has trivial \(T\)-action. Its affine-line and point filtration, with the normalized supported point class of Lesson 6 A.1 and the compact affine trace, gives the two actual ordinary basis classes \(1,h\). Above degree two its ordinary cohomology is zero, so \(h^2=0\); the trivial equivariant action tensors this calculation with \(S\). Equivalently this is the relative projective Chern basis of C.14 for the trivial rank-two bundle. The actual trace gives \[ H_T^*(\mathbb P^1)=S[h]/(h^2),\qquad \pi_*h=1,\quad\pi_*1=0. \tag{C.21.11} \] The reference line has class \(\chi(u)\). Restriction of \(q\) along the torus classifying map is its actual polynomial in the character Chern classes, by C.14. Hence the two curve characteristic classes differ by \[ q(u-\lambda h)-q(u) =-D_\lambda q(u)\,h. \tag{C.21.12} \] Expansion of each polynomial monomial proves the equality, since \(h^2=0\). Taking the negative trace in (C.21.8) proves (C.21.9). The trace removes the \(E(1)\) of \(h\), leaving exactly \(E(d-1)\). No character, coefficient variable or lattice index is inverted. ∎

C.21.4. The higher operators are the full centralizer gradients

Theorem C.21.4.1. Let \(q_1,\ldots,q_r\) be homogeneous polynomial generators of \(R_0\), of degrees \(d_i\), and put \(f_i=q_i\circ\beta_\rho^{-1}\) on the dual adjoint quotient. In the adapted frame C.20.4, the actual \(z_q\)-operator is \[ \nabla_{B_H}(q\circ\beta_\rho^{-1}) \bigl(\sigma_\rho(\beta_\rho(u)\bmod W)\bigr). \tag{C.21.13} \] In particular the \(z_{q_i}\) act by the entire basis of the full centralizer Lie bundle in B.5. They give the geometric module \(\omega_G(IC_\lambda)\) exactly the Kostant presentation \[ M(V_\lambda)=R_0\otimes_E V_\lambda,\qquad z_{q_i}\cdot v= \rho_{V_\lambda}(\nabla_{B_H}f_i(\sigma_\rho))v, \quad |z_{q_i}|=2d_i-2. \tag{C.21.14} \] The polynomial algebra \(\mathcal B_\rho=R_0[z_{q_1},\ldots,z_{q_r}]\) embeds in the actual whole equivariant cohomology ring; equality with that whole ring is not asserted.

Proof. Theorem C.21.2.1 and C.18.4 express cup with \(z_q\) as a homogeneous Lie element \(\eta_q\) of total degree \(2d-2\), retaining the Tate line. Cups of these even-degree classes commute with the determinant cup. Reconstruction identifies their commutator with \([\eta_q,\xi_\rho]\), so that bracket is zero. Thus in the adapted slice frame \(\eta_q\) is a section of the full centralizer Lie bundle.

Extend to the fraction field \(Q\) of \(S\) and then to an algebraic closure of \(Q\). The vector \(\beta_\rho(u)\) is regular semisimple: its root values are nonzero linear polynomials, since \(\beta_\rho\) is invertible. C.20.3 and B.3 identify \(\xi_\rho\) with its geometric adjoint orbit. Choose a conjugator taking it to \(\beta_\rho(u)\). The corresponding \(\eta_q\) then belongs to \(\mathfrak t_H\).

In every \(V_\lambda\), the eigenvalues of this Cartan vector \(\beta_\rho(u)\) distinguish its distinct weights. Therefore its \(\mu\)-weight blocks are precisely the transported actual fixed-point blocks of C.19.4. Actual cup restriction and C.21.9 show that \(\eta_q\) acts on such a block by \(D_\mu q(u)\). Lemma C.21.1.1 gives exactly the same scalar for the gradient in (C.21.13). The weights of a faithful \(H\)-representation span its full character lattice by the all-kernel argument of Lesson 2, Lemma 7.10. Decomposing that representation into its genuine simple modules proves that these scalar equalities determine the Cartan Lie element, including its centre. The two elements agree over the extended fraction field.

Invariant gradients are equivariant under \(H\), by invariance of \(B_H\) and the chain rule. Conjugating back consequently identifies \(\eta_q\) with the gradient at the slice point. Both are sections of the finite free module \(R_0\otimes_E\mathfrak h\). Its injection into the extended fraction-field module makes this equality hold already over \(R_0\), proving (C.21.13) on every parameter algebra.

The \(f_i\) are polynomial generators of the dual invariant ring by C.20.2. Their differentials form the cotangent basis of the quotient along the polynomial slice isomorphism. B.5 therefore makes their gradients the entire Lie-centralizer basis, with the specified total degrees. This proves the actual action and grading in (C.21.14). The full centre supplies its degree-zero gradient operators when \(d_i=1\).

For completeness these actual cup classes have no polynomial relation over \(R_0\). A relation would act as zero on every \(IC_\lambda\), and hence on every localized fixed-point block. Each label \(\mu\in L\) occurs: its dominant Weyl translate has that extremal weight with multiplicity one, and C.20.1 supplies the corresponding nonzero block. At the generic regular Cartan the map \[ \mu\longmapsto(D_\mu q_1(u),\ldots,D_\mu q_r(u)) \tag{C.21.15} \] extends to an invertible linear map over \(Q\); equivalently the quotient differentials are independent there, as checked in B.2. The integral lattice \(L\) is polynomially dense in its affine space: in a lattice basis, repeated one-variable infinite-root arguments prove this for the integer grid. Its image under that invertible map is equally dense. The proposed relation, evaluated at (C.21.15), is therefore the zero polynomial in its operator variables over \(Q\). Injectivity \(R_0\to Q\) makes every coefficient zero already over \(R_0\). This proves the claimed embedding. It leaves the whole ring's degreewise component products intact. ∎

C.21.5. Morphisms over the actual whole cohomology ring

Theorem C.21.5.1. For two spherical IC generators, actual cup linearity gives \[ \operatorname{Hom}_{H_G^*(\operatorname{Gr}_G)}^* (\omega_G(IC_\lambda),\omega_G(IC_\mu)) =\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal B_\rho}^* (M(V_\lambda),M(V_\mu)). \tag{C.21.16} \] Consequently C.16 and B.5 give an actual composition-preserving graded comparison \[ \operatorname{Ext}_{L^+G}^*(IC_\lambda,IC_\mu) \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ } \operatorname{Hom}_{A,H}^* (A\otimes V_\lambda,A\otimes V_\mu), \qquad A=E[\mathfrak h^*], \tag{C.21.17} \] with the deformation grading and full group of B.5. This is the geometric generator module and morphism comparison. An enhanced monoidal equivalence and dg formality remain separate requirements.

Proof. Write \(A_G=H_G^*(\operatorname{Gr}_G)\). Since the \(z_{q_i}\) are actual cup classes, every \(A_G\)-linear map is \(\mathcal B_\rho\)-linear. Conversely let \(F\) be \(\mathcal B_\rho\)-linear. The section \(\xi_\rho=\sigma_\rho\) commutes with itself and hence belongs to the full Lie-centralizer bundle. Its basis from C.21.4 writes it as an \(R_0\)-linear combination of the gradient operators. Thus \(F\) commutes with the actual determinant operator.

Extend \(F\) along the actual scalar comparison \(R_0\to S\) of C.14, then to \(Q\). The determinant eigenvalues \(\mu(\beta_\rho(u))\) distinguish labels, so \(F_Q\) preserves each fixed-point block. Every class of the whole \(A_T=H_T^*(\operatorname{Gr}_G)\) acts on that block by its scalar restriction at \(t^\mu\). This follows from the actual fixed-point restriction isomorphism of C.19.4, which is a module map for those whole classes. The scalar is the same on source and target blocks. Hence \(F_Q\) commutes with every such cup class.

The underlying source and target torus cohomology modules are finite free over \(S\) by C.17. A matrix commutator which becomes zero over \(Q\) is already zero over \(S\). In particular \(F\otimes_{R_0}S\) commutes with the restrictions of every class of \(A_G\). The faithful flatness of \(S/R_0\) makes the original commutator zero. Thus \(F\) is \(A_G\)-linear, proving (C.21.16). This uses scalar actions on the actual generic blocks, not a polynomial presentation of the whole ind-Grassmannian cohomology.

C.16.4 identifies the left side with the original finite-jet spherical generator Ext, through the actual cup comparison preserving Yoneda composition. C.21.4 identifies the right side with the exact modules of B.5, using \(B_H\) to identify the coadjoint coordinates and using the adapted pinning automorphism from C.20.4. Both preserve the original full group. B.5 then gives the right side of (C.21.17), including its specified grading and composition. Composing these isomorphisms proves (C.21.17). None of these graded Hom isomorphisms asserts a dg lift or eliminates higher composition obstructions. ∎

C.21.6. Two derivative and centre exercises

Exercise C.21.6.1 (basic). For \(G=\mathbb G_m\), take \(R_0=E[u]\), label \(n\), and \(q=u^d\). Compute \(z_q\), including \(d=1\), and check primitivity on labels \(n,m\).

Solution. The curve line at that label has class \(u-nh\), with \(h^2=0\). Thus \(-\pi_*((u-nh)^d-u^d)=dn\,u^{d-1}\). Its degree is \(2d-2\), with twist \(E(d-1)\). At \(d=1\) it is the degree-zero label operator \(n\), obtained without dividing by \(u\). At label \(n+m\) its value is \(d(n+m)u^{d-1}=dn u^{d-1}+dm u^{d-1}\), the actual tensor rule.

Exercise C.21.6.2 (intermediate). For \(G=GL_2\), use \(q_1=u_1+u_2\) and \(q_2=u_1u_2\). On the standard minuscule module with determinant cup matrix \(M\) of C.19.18, compute the two higher operators and verify the polynomial relation expressing \(M\) in their span.

Solution. The two labels are \((1,0)\) and \((0,1)\). Their \(q_1\)-derivatives are both one, while their \(q_2\)-derivatives are respectively \(u_2,u_1\). Therefore the two operators are \(I\) and \(\sigma_1I-M\). Equality holds over \(R_0\): localization computes their two distinct eigenspaces, and finite freeness descends the matrix equality. Explicitly \(M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-\sigma_2\\1&\sigma_1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), so \(z_{q_2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma_1&\sigma_2\\-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). The standard trace form gives \(\nabla q_1=I\) and \(\nabla q_2(X)=(\operatorname{tr}X)I-X\), exactly these matrices. Finally \(q_{\rho,2}=\tfrac12(u_1^2+u_2^2)=q_1^2/2-q_2\) has gradient \(X\), and its actual derivative operator is \(\sigma_1 z_{q_1}-z_{q_2}=M\). The degree-zero central operator is essential to this integral formula.

C.22. Tensor generators, their enhanced envelope and arithmetic purity

For the arithmetic statements in this section work over \(k=\overline{\mathbf F}_q\), with finite characteristic-zero coefficient field \(E\) containing \(a\) such that \(a^2=q\). The underlying split group and all the rootwise constructions descend to the finite field. Taking a square power of its prime field makes \(a\) a rational integer, if that is needed to keep the original coefficient field. Let \(A_\lambda\) denote the raw geometric IC with its canonical Weil map multiplied by \(a^{-d_\lambda}\). This is a normalization of the Weil map; it changes neither the underlying geometric object nor its cohomological shift. In the statements not mentioning Frobenius, retain the general geometric coefficient scope of C.21.

Write \[ A=\operatorname{Sym}_E(\mathfrak h[-2]). \tag{C.22.1} \] Thus each linear polynomial has cohomological degree two and the differential is zero. Put a representation \(V\) in degree zero in the free equivariant dg module \(F_V=A\otimes V\). On slice restriction, its grading is the coefficient-plus-principal grading proved in B.5. The notation \(\operatorname{Perf}^H(A)\) means the finite shift, cone and retract envelope of these free modules, exactly as in §8.1.

The first panel distinguishes the actual graded tensor comparison from the enhanced arithmetic equivalence of finite envelopes. The second records the normalized IC Weil maps \(A_\lambda\), the identity action on multiplicities and the sole Ext eigenvalues; the diagram's IC labels denote those same normalized maps. The third gives the strict, locally finite hypothesis and the exact weight-versus-degree truncation. The target keeps the full \(H\), with polynomial coordinates in cohomological degree two. Proofs C.22.1.1–C.22.4.1 establish these maps. Exercise C.22.5.2 shows why cohomological purity alone does not authorize a termwise locally finite replacement. Editable SVG source.

C.22.1. The actual tensor comparison on every generator morphism

Theorem C.22.1.1. Let \(\mathcal E_G^\oplus\) be the graded category of spherical perverse objects, with their actual spherical Ext as morphisms and the corrected Satake convolution symmetry. The comparisons of C.21 extend to a graded symmetric monoidal equivalence \[ \Phi:\mathcal E_G^\oplus\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal F_H, \qquad P\longmapsto F_{\omega(P)}, \tag{C.22.2} \] where \(\mathcal F_H\) has the free modules \(F_V\), their genuine graded \(A,H\)-linear Hom, and tensor over \(A\). For homogeneous generator morphisms \(b,c\), \[ \Phi(b*c)=\Phi(b)\otimes_A\Phi(c). \tag{C.22.3} \] This is a comparison of the actual graded tensor operations. It does not yet identify the enhanced convolution functor.

Proof. Semisimplicity of the geometric spherical heart makes every object a finite sum of IC generators. Thus C.21.5 extends by matrices to a fully faithful graded functor with the displayed objects. Every finite-dimensional representation of the original \(H\) is a finite sum of its true simple modules, by Lesson 11, so these objects give its whole target.

The required naturality is for derived morphisms, rather than just degree-zero maps in the heart. Choose common finite supports and jet bounds for \(b:P\to P'[n]\) and \(c:Q\to Q'[m]\). Genuine equivariant pullback and descent give their external map on the full moving ordered chain. Its target is the moving coefficient for \(P',Q'\), shifted by \(n+m\). The proper images of both coefficient complexes lie in the affine pullback essential image of C.17.3, since the proved constant-cohomology property is unchanged by shifts. The actual affine counits therefore transport this map uniquely on every action-nerve level, including the action face, and on all finite block diagrams.

At distinct points its derived sections are the actual external product map. At collision they are the actual convolution map. The external cup comparison in C.17.2 is functorial for these coefficient maps: its construction uses the tensor of representatives, the projection comparison and composed proper counits; each commutes with a coefficient morphism, with the usual tensor sign. Therefore \[ \omega_G(b*c)=\omega_G(b)\otimes_{R_0}\omega_G(c) \tag{C.22.4} \] under the actual tensor isomorphism C.17.4. In particular the argument did not require an arbitrarily chosen lift of a morphism to fusion.

There is no hidden odd sign on the unshifted generator category. Distinct component supports have zero Ext. Within one component the IC dimensions have the same parity, since a simple coroot changes the dimension height by two. Their star and costalk cell coefficients thus have matching parity. The actual comparison C.16, with the even coefficient ring of C.10, makes their odd Ext zero. Homogeneous nonzero \(b,c\) have even degrees. The shifted finite envelope will use the usual dg signs.

The frame of C.20.4 is tensor on objects and on the heart. Substituting it in (C.22.4) makes the slice module map the coefficient tensor of the two maps in C.21. This equals restriction of their coherent tensor product: on every slice point it is literally the matrix tensor product over the common coefficient ring. The full faithfulness in B.5 makes the inverse polynomial extension equal to that coherent tensor map, proving (C.22.3).

The same tensor frame carries the associator, unit and corrected symmetry on the heart to the usual associator, unit and flip of representations. These are the degree-zero structural maps in the graded category. The genuine finite-set comparisons of C.17 preserve every pentagon, unit and permutation identity, and B.5 preserves their compositions. Hence all diagrams for a graded symmetric monoidal functor commute. This proves (C.22.2) with its actual structural maps. ∎

C.22.2. The enhanced arithmetic free-generator envelope

Theorem C.22.2.1. At the stated arithmetic coefficients and geometric base, there is an equivalence of enhanced categories \[ D^b_{L^+G,\mathrm{sph}}(\operatorname{Gr}_G,E) \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Perf}^H(A) \tag{C.22.5} \] sending every IC generator to \(A\otimes V_\lambda\). The left category has bounded, finite-supported objects. This theorem does not assert that the chosen enhanced equivalence is monoidal, or that it has been transferred to complex coefficients.

Proof. The actual arithmetic formality and generator envelope A.4 identify the left side with the finite-cone and retract envelope of the graded Ext category with zero differential. Its hypotheses are the split finite-field geometry, the actual IC stalk/costalk Frobenius of Lesson 12 C.3, and the enhanced Frobenius lift in A.4. The nonresonance proof A.3 permits its Jordan parts. C.22.1 identifies that formal graded category, including composition, with \(\mathcal F_H\).

We check that its envelope is the asserted dg module envelope, rather than invoking an ordinary coherent derived category in its place. For a rational \(H\)-equivariant dg \(A\)-module \(M\), \[ \operatorname{Hom}_{A,H}^\bullet(F_V,M) =(V^\vee\otimes_E M)^H . \tag{C.22.6} \] Invariants of rational \(H\)-modules are exact in characteristic zero. For an arbitrary rational module this follows from the finite-dimensional proof: an element and a chosen lift lie in finite-dimensional \(H\)-stable spans, and complete reducibility splits the resulting finite image. An invariant image vector consequently has an invariant lift. Applying this to the cycles and boundaries in an acyclic dg module proves that (C.22.6) is acyclic whenever \(M\) is acyclic. Thus each \(F_V\) computes its derived Hom directly.

In particular the actual dg Hom between two free objects is \[ \operatorname{Hom}_{A,H}^\bullet(F_V,F_W) =(A\otimes V^\vee\otimes W)^H \tag{C.22.7} \] with differential zero, the very graded category \(\mathcal F_H\). There are no extra rational group cohomology terms. Its enriched Yoneda comparison is an isomorphism on these free generators, with their actual composition. In each variable it extends through shifts and cones by the corresponding mapping-cone identity, and through retracts by the image of a commuting idempotent. This proves an enhanced equivalence of its finite envelope with the finite shift, cone and retract subcategory of equivariant dg \(A\)-modules. That subcategory is our definition of \(\operatorname{Perf}^H(A)\).

Composing this equivalence with A.4 and C.22.1 gives (C.22.5), with the specified generator images. Although C.22.1 is monoidal on the graded generators, the formality coordinate change in A.4 was constructed for composition. A comparison of the enhanced convolution bifunctor and its coherent structural maps is still needed to make this chosen equivalence monoidal. ∎

C.22.3. Scalar Frobenius on ordinary IC cohomology and tensor multiplicities

Theorem C.22.3.1. The actual normalized ordinary cohomology of \(A_\lambda\) has scalar Frobenius \[ F|_{H^n(\operatorname{Gr}_G,A_\lambda)} =a^n\operatorname{id}. \tag{C.22.8} \] For any finite convolution word \(P=A_{\lambda_1}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_r}\), every multiplicity space in its canonical geometric isotypic decomposition \[ P=\bigoplus_\nu A_\nu\otimes_E M_\nu(P), \qquad M_\nu(P)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Perv}}(A_\nu,P), \tag{C.22.9} \] has the identity Frobenius. Consequently every degree-\(n\) Ext between normalized convolution words has sole eigenvalue \(a^n\), with Jordan parts allowed. Associators, units and the corrected permutations on these words retain their actual arithmetic structures.

Proof. The rootwise source \(\mathcal B_\lambda\) of Lesson 11 C.2.1 is an iterated wall projective-line bundle, beginning with a point, defined over \(\mathbf F_q\). On each last line choose the global character line of C.16.1, whose fibre degree is a nonzero integer \(-m\). The class \(-c_1(\mathcal L)/m\) has fibre trace one. The coefficient field has characteristic zero, so this division is allowed even when the geometric wall central kernel is nonsmooth.

For a wall projection \(p\), its classes \(1,-c_1(\mathcal L)/m\) give the actual proper-image comparison \[ E\oplus E(-1)[-2]\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }Rp_*E. \tag{C.22.10} \] Indeed on every geometric fibre \(\mathbb P^1\) they give its two independent normalized basis classes; actual proper base change checks the cone at those fibres. The line and divisor transitions are defined over the finite field. Kummer Frobenius sends its untwisted degree-two class to \(q\) times that class. Thus (C.22.10) is compatible with the two displayed Frobenius scalars.

Iterate (C.22.10). Products of its degree-two classes give an actual basis of \(H^{2j}(\mathcal B_\lambda,E)\), on which Frobenius is \(q^j\) times the identity, and the odd groups vanish. For the raw resolution image \(B_\lambda=Rf_{\lambda*}E[d_\lambda]\), its ordinary degree-\(n\) cohomology therefore has scalar \(q^{(n+d_\lambda)/2}\).

Use the actual underlying IC retractions \(I_\lambda\xrightarrow{u}B_\lambda\xrightarrow{v}I_\lambda\), with \(vu=1\) and both dense restrictions the identity, proved in Lesson 11 C.4.2. The compression proof of Lesson 12 C.2 applies also to ordinary cohomology on this proper support. Here is its required transport. If \(s:X\to\operatorname{Spec}k\) is the support map, the field square \(s\sigma_X=\sigma_ks\), actual proper base change and pullback composition identify \(R\Gamma(X,\sigma_X^*K)\) with the pullback of \(R\Gamma(X,K)\) to the geometric field point. Field pullback on its coefficient-module category is canonically the identity, naturally for every underlying derived map.

The composite \(v\alpha_{B_\lambda}\sigma^*u\) is the canonical raw IC Weil map: it has the same identity on the dense cell, and intermediate extension excludes an endomorphism with boundary-supported image. With the just-specified global transport, its degree-\(n\) action is therefore \[ H^n(v)\,F_{B_\lambda}\,H^n(u) =q^{(n+d_\lambda)/2}\operatorname{id}. \tag{C.22.11} \] No separate arithmetic equivariance of \(u,v\) was assumed. Multiplication of the Weil map by \(a^{-d_\lambda}\) gives (C.22.8).

The actual ordinary cohomology tensor comparison of Lesson 8 and C.17 is arithmetic-compatible: all its gluing, proper maps, normalization traces and external cup maps descend to the finite field. Hence the degree-\(n\) cohomology of \(P\) also has scalar \(a^n\), as a tensor of the scalars in (C.22.8).

The geometric semisimple heart gives the canonical evaluation isomorphism (C.22.9). It is arithmetic-equivariant when \(M_\nu(P)\) has the conjugation action defined by the two Weil maps. Frobenius fixes the labels, since their rootwise data are split. Applying \(H^n\) gives a direct sum of \(H^n(A_\nu)\otimes M_\nu(P)\). For each occurring \(\nu\), choose \(n\) with \(H^n(A_\nu)\ne0\); such an \(n\) exists by the proved faithfulness of total cohomology. On this summand the two scalar formulas force \(a^n(1\otimes F_{M_\nu})=a^n1\). Tensoring with a nonzero finite-dimensional vector space is faithful, so \(F_{M_\nu}=1\), proving the assertion even against a possible Jordan part.

Decompose both convolution words by (C.22.9). Their actual Ext is the finite sum of IC Ext tensored with coefficient Hom between identity-Frobenius multiplicities. A.2 gives its sole eigenvalue \(a^n\). The structural maps mentioned in the theorem were actual finite-field fusion maps in Lesson 8; normalization uses the same scalar on every ordered input. They consequently retain their descended arithmetic structures under this isotypic description. ∎

C.22.4. A multiplicative truncation when the cochain action is locally finite

Theorem C.22.4.1. Let \(C\) be a dg category, dg algebra, or diagram of complexes over \(E\), with a strict chain automorphism \(F\), fixing every object in the category case. Suppose \(F\) is locally finite on every cochain vector, all its eigenvalues are among \(a^w\) for integers \(w\), and \(a\) is not a root of unity. Suppose \[ H^n(C)\text{ has sole eigenvalue }a^n. \tag{C.22.12} \] Its composition and specified degree-zero multilinear structure maps commute strictly with \(F\). Then there is a natural multiplicative zigzag \[ C\ \longleftarrow\ D(C)\ \longrightarrow\ H^*(C) \tag{C.22.13} \] of quasi-isomorphisms, retaining the ordinary graded structure. For a strictly \(F\)-equivariant higher operation of degree \(-r<0\), its restriction to \(D(C)\) lands in \(D(C)\) and its projection to \(H^*(C)\) is zero. The locally finite and strict cochain hypotheses in this statement must be verified before applying it to an enhanced geometric diagram.

Proof. Local finiteness means that each vector lies in a finite-dimensional \(F\)-stable space. Primary decomposition there, followed by its unique compatible inclusions, gives the canonical direct sum \[ C=\bigoplus_{w\in\mathbb Z}C_w,\qquad C_w=\{x:(F-a^w)^N x=0\text{ for some }N\}. \tag{C.22.14} \] Distinct integer weights have distinct eigenvalues. The differential preserves these subspaces. Products add their weights: on finite-dimensional primary spaces the tensor automorphism has sole eigenvalue \(a^{w+v}\), since its remaining part is a polynomial without constant term in commuting nilpotent operators. Every \(F\)-equivariant multilinear map therefore sends input weights to their sum.

Define, in cohomological degree \(n\), \[ D(C)^n= \bigoplus_{w>n}C_w^n \ \oplus\ \ker(d:C_n^n\to C_n^{n+1}). \tag{C.22.15} \] Weights below \(n\) are omitted. This is a subcomplex. If \(w>n+1\), the differential remains in the full \(w\)-summand; if \(w=n+1\), it lands in the diagonal cycle term; the differential of a diagonal cycle is zero. For a fixed weight \(w\) it is exactly the good subcomplex truncation \(\tau_{\leq w}C_w\). Equation (C.22.12) says that \(H^n(C_w)=0\) for \(n\ne w\), so its inclusion into \(C_w\) is a quasi-isomorphism. Direct sums of vector-space complexes are exact, proving the left arrow in (C.22.13).

The right arrow takes a diagonal cycle to its cohomology class and every term of weight \(w>n\) to zero. It is a chain map: an off-diagonal differential can reach the diagonal only as an ordinary boundary. On the sole weight-\(w\) cohomology group it is its defining quotient map, so it too is a quasi-isomorphism.

If two inputs have weights at least their degrees, their product has that property. When both weights equal the degrees, both inputs are cycles and the degree-zero chain product is a cycle. Thus (C.22.15) is closed under composition and the degree-zero chain structure maps. The right arrow preserves them: any strict weight excess on an input leaves an excess on the output, and on diagonal cycles it is ordinary cohomological composition. Units lie in degree and weight zero, and are retained. These observations also prove naturality in strictly \(F\)-equivariant chain maps.

Tensor comparisons are compatible as well. There is a natural product map \(D(C)\otimes D(C')\to D(C\otimes C')\); both paths to \(C\otimes C'\) are the inclusion of the same tensor of cochains. Tensoring quasi-isomorphisms over a field preserves them, even for unbounded complexes: an acyclic vector-space complex has a contracting homotopy by complements to its cycles, and tensoring that homotopy contracts its tensor with any complex. The two inclusion maps are quasi-isomorphisms, so this product comparison is one. The same multiplication of representatives proves its associative and permutation identities.

Finally an operation of degree \(-r\) sends inputs of degrees \(n_i\) and weights \(w_i\ge n_i\) to output degree \(\sum n_i-r\) and weight \(\sum w_i\). For \(r>0\) that output has strictly larger weight than degree. It belongs to the full off-diagonal term of \(D(C)\), and its cohomology projection is zero. Restriction thus retains any supplied operation identities; projection kills their negative-degree terms. Jordan parts have been retained throughout by primary spaces, not discarded by diagonalization. ∎

C.22.5. Two cochain-model exercises

Exercise C.22.5.1 (intermediate). Let \(C=E[x,z]\otimes\Lambda(y)\), with \(|x|=|z|=2,|y|=1\), \(dy=z\) and \(dx=dz=0\). Give all three generators weight two and let \(F\) act by \(a^2\) on them. Compute \(D(C)\) and its map to cohomology.

Solution. The polynomial generators give diagonal cycle monomials \(x^i z^j\); the monomials \(yx^i z^j\) have weight one above their cohomological degree. These exhaust the basis, so \(D(C)=C\). Their differentials are \(x^i z^{j+1}\). Thus every monomial containing \(z\) is a boundary, the odd terms have no cycles, and \(H^*(C)=E[x]\). The right arrow sends \(x\) to \(x\), \(z,y\) to zero. It is multiplicative and retains the weight-two class of \(x\). The calculation demonstrates why diagonal boundaries are killed, while an off-diagonal primitive is retained until the projection.

Exercise C.22.5.2 (advanced). In degrees zero and one take \(C^0=C^1=E[t,t^{-1}]\), differential multiplication by \(t-a\), and chain automorphism multiplication by \(t\). Compute its cohomology and show why taking the locally finite subspace of each term is not a valid replacement.

Solution. The differential is injective in the Laurent polynomial domain and has cokernel \(E\), by evaluation at \(t=a\). Thus \(H^0=0\), and \(H^1=E\) with Frobenius \(a=a^1\), exactly diagonal purity. Yet every nonzero Laurent polynomial has linearly independent translates among sufficiently many powers of \(t\): a finite polynomial relation among them would contradict the domain property. There is no nonzero locally finite vector in either term. Taking those termwise subspaces gives the zero complex and loses \(H^1\). A geometric application must construct a compatible derived locally finite model, rather than making that termwise substitution from cohomological purity alone.

C.23. Derived primary replacement and coherent arithmetic formality

We retain the arithmetic field and normalized Weil objects of §22. Put \[ R=E[t,t^{-1}],\qquad K=E(t),\qquad \mathcal W=\{t-a^w:w\in\mathbb Z\}. \tag{C.23.1} \] An \(R\)-module is the same thing as an \(E\)-vector space with an invertible operator \(F=t\). In every tensor product in this section the base is \(E\), and \(t\) acts diagonally, \(F_{M\otimes N}=F_M\otimes F_N\). It is not a tensor product over \(R\). Let \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\) be the full abelian category of modules that are locally finite for \(F\) and have only the primary eigenvalues \(a^w\). Thus \[ M=\bigoplus_{w\in\mathbb Z} \bigcup_{r\ge1}\ker(F-a^w)^r. \tag{C.23.2} \] The Jordan blocks in (C.23.2) are allowed. A bounded-below derived object will mean one with cohomology zero below some integer; it need not be presented initially by a bounded-below cochain complex.

The free corrected author version of Cirici and Horel, Étale cohomology, purity and formality with torsion coefficients, §§2 and 4, explains the distinction between locally finite cochains and locally finite cohomology. Here the coefficient field has characteristic zero and \(a\) has infinite multiplicative order. We prove the required derived replacement and coherence statement below; no theorem from that paper stands in place of a proof.

C.23.1. Resolve first, then take primary vectors

Theorem C.23.1.1. There is a natural bounded-below complex \(L(C)\) in \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\), for every bounded-below complex \(C\) of \(R\)-modules whose cohomology lies in \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\), and a natural zigzag \[ C\longrightarrow J(C)\longleftarrow L(C) \tag{C.23.3} \] of quasi-isomorphisms. Both \(J(C)\) and \(L(C)\) have injective \(R\)-modules in each term. In particular, this construction applies to the Laurent-polynomial complex of Exercise C.22.5.2, although its original terms have no locally finite vectors.

Proof. The ring \(R\) is a principal ideal domain: every ideal is obtained by localizing an ideal of the Euclidean polynomial ring \(E[t]\). An \(R\)-module is locally finite precisely when it is torsion. In one direction a finite-dimensional invariant subspace gives an annihilating polynomial with nonzero constant coefficient, since \(F\) is invertible. In the other direction \(Rv\), annihilated by a nonzero Laurent polynomial, is a quotient of a finite-dimensional vector space \(R/(p)\).

We need two elementary facts about injectivity. An injective \(R\)-module \(I\) is divisible: a map \((p)\to I\) sending \(p\) to a specified element extends to \(R\). Conversely a divisible module is injective. To prove the converse, extend a map on a submodule to a maximal larger domain by the union of chains. If \(x\) is outside that domain, the ideal \(\{r:rx\text{ belongs to the domain}\}\) is either zero or \((p)\). In the zero case assign any value to \(x\). In the second case choose \(y\) with \(py\) equal to the already prescribed value of \(px\). Then \(f(n+rx)=f(n)+ry\) is well defined and extends the map, contradicting maximality. This proves injectivity without invoking a criterion.

Write \(\Gamma_{\rm tor}I\) for the torsion submodule of an injective module. It is divisible: if \(px=v\) in \(I\) and \(qv=0\), then \(qpx=0\), so \(x\) is torsion. It is therefore itself injective. The quotient \(I/\Gamma_{\rm tor}I\) is canonically \(K\otimes_R I\). The kernel of the localization map is precisely torsion; surjectivity follows from divisibility, which supplies division by every nonzero denominator. We have consequently proved \[ 0\longrightarrow\Gamma_{\rm tor}I \longrightarrow I\longrightarrow K\otimes_R I \longrightarrow0. \tag{C.23.4} \]

Torsion modules have their canonical primary direct sum decomposition. Indeed an element has an annihilating polynomial with finitely many irreducible factors; Bézout identities for the mutually coprime factor powers give its unique primary pieces. These identities agree when one enlarges an annihilating polynomial. Projection onto the summands in \(\mathcal W\), denoted \(\Gamma_{\mathcal W}\), is exact on torsion modules. Also \(\Gamma_{\mathcal W}I\) is divisible: choose a torsion solution of \(px=v\) as just above, and project that solution onto its \(\mathcal W\)-primary summands. The projection commutes with \(p\) and fixes \(v\). Thus \(\Gamma_{\mathcal W}I\) too is injective over \(R\).

Here is a functorial resolution, including the boundedness needed to totalize it. For an \(E\)-vector space \(V\), put \[ I(V)=\operatorname{Hom}_E(R,V),\qquad (t f)(t^j)=f(t^{j+1}). \tag{C.23.5} \] If \(U\) forgets the \(R\)-action, evaluation at \(1\) identifies \(\operatorname{Hom}_R(M,I(V))=\operatorname{Hom}_E(UM,V)\). The latter functor is exact, so \(I(V)\) is injective. Let \(T=IU\), with unit \(\eta_M(m)(r)=rm\) and multiplication \(I\epsilon U:T^2\to T\), where \(\epsilon\) is evaluation at \(1\).

The augmented cosimplicial resolution of \(M\) has term \(T^{p+1}M\) in degree \(p\ge0\); its cofaces insert \(\eta\) in each position, and its codegeneracies apply \(I\epsilon U\). The unit and multiplication identities give the cosimplicial identities. After applying \(U\), evaluation at the outermost \(1\) is an extra codegeneracy \(e\). If \(d^i\) is a coface, then \(ed^0=1\) and \(ed^i=d^{i-1}e\) for \(i>0\). Taking the alternating sum gives \(e\delta+\delta e=1\) on the augmented complex. Thus it is exact.

Apply this construction degreewise to \(C\), whose terms may be taken zero in degrees below \(b\). Totalize with \[ J(C)^n=\bigoplus_{\substack{p\ge0\\p+q=n}} T^{p+1}C^q,\qquad d_{\rm tot}=\delta+(-1)^p d_C. \tag{C.23.6} \] For each \(n\) the sum has at most \(n-b+1\) nonzero terms. Every term is injective and the total complex is bounded below. The preceding extra codegeneracy contracts the augmented horizontal direction; its cancellation with the vertical differential follows from the signs in (C.23.6). Equivalently filter by the bounded finite diagonals: horizontal exactness says that \(C\to J(C)\) induces an isomorphism on cohomology. This proves the first quasi-isomorphism in (C.23.3).

Apply (C.23.4) termwise to \(J(C)\). Localization is exact, so \[ H^n(K\otimes_R J(C))=K\otimes_R H^n(C)=0. \tag{C.23.7} \] The exact sequence of complexes therefore proves \(\Gamma_{\rm tor}J(C)\to J(C)\) a quasi-isomorphism. The primary decomposition of the torsion complex is a direct sum of subcomplexes. Exactness of taking primary summands shows that its summands outside \(\mathcal W\) have zero cohomology, because \(H^n(C)\) belongs to \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\). Hence \[ L(C)=\Gamma_{\mathcal W}J(C) \tag{C.23.8} \] has the claimed inclusion and cohomology. Its terms are injective over \(R\) by the divisibility argument. All constructions commute with \(R\)-linear cochain maps. The resolution is taken before (C.23.8); no assertion has been made about \(\Gamma_{\mathcal W}C\) in an arbitrary original cochain model. ∎

C.23.2. The replacement retains tensor coherence

Let \(D^+_{\mathcal W}(R)\) be the full enhanced derived category of bounded-below \(R\)-complexes with cohomology in \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\). “Enhanced” here retains mapping complexes and their homotopies, rather than just the degree-zero morphisms in a triangulated category. The tensor product is the diagonal \(E\)-tensor product specified after (C.23.1).

Theorem C.23.2.1. Inclusion induces a symmetric monoidal equivalence of enhanced derived categories \[ D^+(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}) \ \xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\ D^+_{\mathcal W}(R). \tag{C.23.9} \] Its inverse retains a complete homotopy-coherent diagram of multilinear maps, including units, associators and permutations. It is not the termwise functor \(\Gamma_{\mathcal W}\) on the original diagram.

Proof. Kernels, cokernels and extensions of modules in \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\) remain in that category. For an extension, lift an element killed by a product of allowed primary powers in the quotient; a second such product kills its image in the submodule, so their product kills the lift. The primary decomposition then proves (C.23.2) for the extension. The category has enough injectives that are also injective over \(R\): embed a module \(M\) in \(I(UM)\) by \(\eta_M\), and take \(\Gamma_{\mathcal W}I(UM)\). Its image contains \(M\); divisibility above proves the target injective over \(R\), hence also in the full subcategory \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\).

A bounded-below complex of injective modules is an injective mapping model. Here is the required argument for unbounded source complexes as well. If \(B\) is acyclic and a cochain map \(f:B\to J\) is given, start below the lowest nonzero term of \(J\). Inductively, after choosing \(h^n:B^n\to J^{n-1}\), the map \(f^n-d_Jh^n\) vanishes on \(\ker d_B^n=\operatorname{im}d_B^{n-1}\), by the cochain-map identity and the preceding induction step. It defines a map on \(\operatorname{im}d_B^n\) with values in \(J^n\). Injectivity extends this to \(h^{n+1}:B^{n+1}\to J^n\). These extensions give \(f=dh+hd\). Apply the same argument to every shift of \(J\). Thus its Hom complex from an acyclic complex is acyclic, which is precisely the mapping-model property.

For two complexes in \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\), resolve the target by these injectives. The very same Hom complex computes its enhanced derived maps in \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\) and in \(R\)-modules: the inclusion is full, and the chosen injectives work in both categories. This proves full faithfulness on mapping complexes, in every degree. Theorem C.23.1.1 proves essential surjectivity. An object initially bounded below only in cohomology may first be replaced by its good quotient truncation; its canonical quasi-isomorphism supplies the same derived object.

The diagonal tensor product preserves \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\). Each finite collection of vectors lies in finite-dimensional primary spaces. On weights \(w,v\), the tensor automorphism has sole eigenvalue \(a^{w+v}\), with a nilpotent remaining part, by the commuting-nilpotent calculation in C.22.4. The tensor product is exact over the field \(E\). It preserves quasi-isomorphisms: a splitting of an acyclic vector-space complex into its boundary pairs supplies a contracting homotopy after tensoring. Tensor products of bounded-below complexes are bounded below. The inclusion in (C.23.9) is therefore an honest symmetric monoidal functor before passing to enhanced derived categories.

We explain explicitly why its inverse retains coherence. The functor \(L\) of (C.23.8) preserves quasi-isomorphisms on this subcategory, and (C.23.3) gives a natural derived equivalence \(\zeta_C:UL(C)\simeq C\). The desired tensor comparison is the lift, by full faithfulness, of \[ \zeta_{C\otimes D}^{-1} (\zeta_C\otimes\zeta_D): UL(C)\otimes UL(D)\longrightarrow UL(C\otimes D). \tag{C.23.10} \] It is an equivalence. The unit comparison is obtained in the same way. This lifting uses the full mapping objects, not only their \(H^0\): the preceding identical mapping complexes make the space of a lift, together with its comparison homotopy, contractible.

Consequently the associativity and permutation homotopies of (C.23.10) lift too. Their next coherence homotopies lift for the same reason, and so on over the dimension of the supplied diagram. More concretely, a discrepancy on the boundary of a chosen higher homotopy is a cycle in the homotopy fibre of the mapping comparison; that fibre is acyclic, so the discrepancy has a primitive. Differences between choices are filled one degree lower in the same fibre. Each fixed finite-dimensional diagram requires finitely many such steps; the constructions agree as that dimension is enlarged. This proves all coherent identities and their compatible lifts. It proves a symmetric monoidal equivalence, without claiming a strictly symmetric tensor product on the particular coinduced total complex (C.23.6). ∎

C.23.3. Diagonal purity formalizes the whole diagram

Call a derived \(R\)-complex diagonally pure if it is bounded below, has degreewise finite-dimensional cohomology, and \(H^n\) has sole generalized eigenvalue \(a^n\). These objects are closed under the diagonal \(E\)-tensor product: in each total degree the Künneth sum is finite, and the input eigenvalues multiply to \(a^{n+m}\).

Theorem C.23.3.1. On the diagonally pure full subcategory, the functor which forgets the \(R\)-action is equivalent, as a symmetric monoidal enhanced functor, to its graded cohomology with zero differential. Therefore a complete diagram of diagonally pure mapping objects with Frobenius-equivariant compositions, tensor maps and structural homotopies is formal together with that entire structure.

Proof. Use (C.23.9) to lift the derived diagram to locally finite primary complexes. This is a symmetric monoidal lift; it is not a replacement of its members independently followed by unspecified new tensor maps. On complexes in \(\mathsf L_{\mathcal W}\) with diagonal cohomology, C.22.4 gives the two natural maps \[ UC\ \longleftarrow\ UD(C)\ \longrightarrow\ H^*(UC), \tag{C.23.11} \] where \[ D(C)^n=\bigoplus_{w>n}C_w^n \oplus\ker(d:C_n^n\to C_n^{n+1}). \tag{C.23.12} \] Both arrows are quasi-isomorphisms, and \(D\) preserves quasi-isomorphisms on these objects because its cohomology map is the defining cohomology isomorphism.

The cochain functor \(D\) is lax symmetric monoidal. Its comparison sends a tensor of representatives to the same tensor in \(D(C\otimes C')\). Input weights at least the input degrees give this inclusion; diagonal inputs are cycles. C.22.4 proved the comparison a quasi-isomorphism. The associative, unit and signed permutation diagrams commute on the representatives themselves. The projection in (C.23.11) is monoidal under the field Künneth isomorphism: diagonal cycles project to their classes, and any positive weight excess projects to zero before or after taking a product. Thus, after passing to derived objects, all comparisons are equivalences and (C.23.11) is a symmetric monoidal equivalence of functors.

Compose this equivalence with the coherent inverse of (C.23.9) and with \(\zeta\). Cohomology of that inverse is canonically the original cohomology by (C.23.3), so the result is exactly the asserted formal functor, with no altered graded multiplication.

For clarity, a coherent algebraic diagram is made of tensor products of its mapping objects, structure maps out of those products, and specified homotopies among composites. Apply the functors and their monoidal comparisons to every member and every such map. Naturality carries each specified homotopy and its higher faces to the corresponding one. Objectwise equivalences give an equivalence of the entire diagram. On the formal output a strictly equivariant operation of degree \(-r<0\) would have to take eigenvalue \(a^{\sum n_i}\) into eigenvalue \(a^{\sum n_i-r}\); these differ, even with Jordan parts. The map is zero. This is also the representative calculation in C.22.4. Ordinary degree-zero structure maps remain their actual graded cohomology maps. The result is simultaneous formality, not merely an unrelated quasi-isomorphism for each Hom complex. ∎

Lemma C.23.3.2. For two diagonally pure objects \(C,D\), every negative-degree derived \(R\)-linear map is zero, and \[ \operatorname{Hom}_{D(R)}(C,D) =\prod_n\operatorname{Hom}_R(H^nC,H^nD). \tag{C.23.13} \] This statement does not assert the vanishing of positive-degree derived \(R\)-linear maps.

Proof. In a locally finite primary model, decompose into its weight subcomplexes. The weight-\(w\) subcomplex has cohomology only in degree \(w\). Its good subcomplex truncation through \(w\), followed by its cycle quotient in degree \(w\), gives a natural quasi-isomorphism zigzag to \(H^w[-w]\). Direct sums are exact. By (C.23.9), this proves in \(D(R)\) \[ C\simeq\bigoplus_n(H^nC)[-n], \qquad D\simeq\bigoplus_m(H^mD)[-m]. \tag{C.23.14} \] For a finite-dimensional module \(M\) with invertible \(F_M\), the following is a free resolution: \[ 0\longrightarrow R\otimes_E M \xrightarrow{\,t\otimes1-1\otimes F_M\,} R\otimes_E M\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow0. \tag{C.23.15} \] The last map sends \(t^j\otimes v\) to \(F_M^jv\). The middle map is injective by comparing the highest Laurent exponent in a nonzero finite sum. In its cokernel the relation \(t\otimes v=1\otimes F_Mv\), and its inverse using \(F_M^{-1}\), reduces every Laurent sum uniquely to \(1\otimes v\). This proves exactness.

Applied to a target module \(N\), its Hom differential, up to a harmless overall sign, is \[ g\longmapsto F_Ng-gF_M. \tag{C.23.16} \] If the two sole eigenvalues are \(a^m,a^n\) with \(m\ne n\), this is invertible: left and right multiplication commute, their nonconstant parts are nilpotent, and their scalar difference is nonzero. Thus both Hom and \(\operatorname{Ext}^1_R(M,N)\) vanish for different weights, and higher Ext vanish by (C.23.15).

The direct sum of the shifted two-term resolutions (C.23.15) is a valid projective mapping model for (C.23.14). Each two-term resolution has acyclic Hom into an acyclic complex, and the Hom of their direct sum is the product of those complexes. Products of exact sequences of modules are exact, by choosing a preimage in each coordinate. Hence that product is acyclic. This argument avoids assuming that an arbitrary bounded-below complex of projectives is such a mapping model.

For the \(n,m\) terms, a degree-\(k\) map is computed by \(\operatorname{Ext}_R^{k+n-m}(H^nC,H^mD)\). The different-weight terms vanish. The same-weight terms can occur only for \(k=0,1\); their \(k=0\) terms give (C.23.13). This proves the lemma. In particular mapping homotopies of positive dimension, which are negative-degree maps, have no nonzero homotopy classes here. Same-weight \(\operatorname{Ext}^1_R\) may still encode a nontrivial Jordan extension. ∎

C.23.4. The actual Weil diagram for convolution words

Let \(\mathcal P_{\rm word}\) contain the normalized IC objects, the unit, their finite direct sums, and every finite ordered convolution word, with all parenthesizations retained.
